- [[Matplotlib Object Model and Seaborn Function Levels]] — Matplotlib is a tree of Figure, Axes and artists with two APIs (use the object-oriented one), and pandas and seaborn draw through it; seaborn splits into figure-level and axes-level functions; plus pandas shortcuts, library-version changes, format and pitfall notes.
- [[Maximum Likelihood and Estimator Bias]] — An estimator is a recipe judged by bias, variance and MSE; maximum likelihood picks the parameter that makes the data most probable, gives standard errors from the curvature of the log-likelihood, and is a little biased at small n.
- [[MC Dropout]] — Keep dropout switched on at test time and average several stochastic forward passes; the paper argues this approximates Bayesian inference in a deep Gaussian process and gives the spread of the passes as a model-uncertainty signal at no change to training.
- [[MCMC and Metropolis Sampling]] — MCMC returns correlated samples from the posterior using only density ratios; step size decides whether a Metropolis chain works, effective sample size and R-hat tell whether it did, and gradient-based NUTS removes the tuning for continuous models.
- [[Medallion Layers Need Tests]] — Bronze, silver and gold layers organise where problems get fixed but detect nothing; filters between layers drop rows silently unless a test counts them.
- [[MemLong Retrieval Memory Layers]] — MemLong caches chunk key-value pairs from a frozen lower-layer stack and retrieves them with an external dense retriever into upper layers; OpenLLaMA-3B kept lowering perplexity past 2048 tokens (self-reported).
- [[Metric Definitions, Exposures and a Manifest Catalog]] — Define each metric once near the models so every tool asks for it, declare dashboards as exposures with owners to extend lineage, and start a catalog from the dbt manifest with a CI gate on missing owners.
- [[Metric Tree Decomposition]] — A metric tree splits a top number into identity edges that can be asserted and attributed exactly (log-mean weights) and dashed causal edges that need a test before anyone acts; a count north star needs a paired check.
- [[Microsoft ML Workflow Survey: Nine Stages and an Activity Maturity Index]] — A Microsoft interview-and-survey study of software teams building ML describes a nine-stage workflow with feedback loops, finds data management the top challenge at every experience level, and proposes a six-dimension maturity index with no defined levels.
- [[MIMESIS User Simulator Training]] — MIMESIS trains a 9B user simulator to behave like real users and uses it as the training environment for interactive agents; an agent trained against it scored higher on nine held-out simulators than one trained against GPT-5.5 (self-reported), and coached self-distillation added a further gain.
- [[MiniMax Sparse Attention]] — MiniMax Sparse Attention (MSA) adds a light index branch that picks 16 blocks of 128 key-value tokens per query group, cutting attention FLOPs 28.4x at 1M tokens versus dense GQA while staying close to full-attention quality at 109B scale; it reduces bytes read, not bytes stored (self-reported).
- [[Minimum Detectable Effect and Sample Size]] — Sample size per arm is about 16 sigma squared over delta squared at 5 percent significance and 80 percent power, so halving the detectable effect quadruples the users and small relative lifts on rare outcomes need millions.
- [[Misleading Axes: Dual Axes and Truncated Bars]] — Two y scales let the author author the story, and a bar from a non-zero baseline exaggerates differences; use aligned panels, an index, and zero-based bars.
- [[Mixture Models and Label Switching]] — A two-cluster mixture can be fitted with sampled discrete labels or, far faster, by summing the labels out of the likelihood; symmetric components then swap identity across chains, which an ordering constraint or informative priors fixes.
- [[Mixture-of-Transformers]] — A multimodal architecture that gives each modality its own non-embedding weights (feed-forward, attention projections, norms) with shared global attention and a fixed modality tag as the router; matches a dense Chameleon 7B with 55.8% of FLOPs (self-reported).
- [[ML Deployment Challenges by Workflow Stage]] — A literature survey of deployment case studies maps issues onto four workflow stages plus cross-cutting concerns, argues that challenges appear at every stage, and ranks none of them.
- [[ML Frame Sourcing — License, Horizon, Prevalence]] — decision: public frames chosen on redistribution license, a time column for temporal split, and prevalence low enough to exercise the sufficiency guard; imbalance ≠ insufficiency
- [[ML Run Reproducibility Bar]] — five-element bar (tests, synthetic-twin dry run, owned guards, run record + reproduce, data sweep) making an ML finding refutable from the record
- [[ML Stage Layering with Typed Contracts]] — organise ML code by process stage with a Pydantic schema per boundary; technique folders become libraries the stages call
- [[ML System Design: Stages and Handoffs]] — Designing an ML system is a chain of stages from framing to correction loop, each handing the next a concrete artefact; the model is one row, the decision and its price come first, and the plain baseline doubles as the fallback.
- [[ML Test Score: Scoring Production Readiness by the Minimum]] — A 28-test rubric in four sections of seven (data, model, infrastructure, monitoring), half a point manual and one automated, scored by the minimum across sections so one strong area cannot hide a gap.
- [[MLOps Maturity Levels and CT vs CI/CD]] — Maturity models ask how much of the ML lifecycle runs without a human; level 4 (monitoring closes the retraining loop) is unsafe without strong monitoring, continuous training and CI/CD are separate loops, and a self-assessed level is a planning aid, not evidence.
- [[Model Compression with MUNGE Mimic Nets]] — Bucila, Caruana and Niculescu-Mizil (KDD 2006) compress a large ensemble into a small neural net trained on ensemble-labelled synthetic data from MUNGE; on eight binary problems the net matched ensemble RMSE (0.264 against 0.263) at about 1000x smaller and faster, and failed on one (self-reported).
- [[Model Registry Aliases and the Run Record]] — A tracker records every run with its data version; a registry names the runs that matter via numbered versions and movable aliases; the registry labels but does not deploy, so a service must resolve the alias and log the version it got.
- [[Model Rollout: Shadow, Canary and A/B]] — A model that won the offline gate has won on old data; shadow shows disagreement before labels exist, canary routes a stable hashed share, and A/B is the only option when predictions change future data.
- [[Model Selection Criteria]] — R-squared never rejects extra terms, adjusted R-squared, AIC, BIC, likelihood-ratio tests and cross-validation each trade fit against complexity differently: BIC found a true quadratic 98 percent of the time at n=800, AIC 77 percent.
- [[ModernBERT Encoder Backbone]] — ModernBERT is an encoder-only model with RoPE, GeGLU, alternating global and local attention and unpadding, trained on 2T tokens with 8192-token context, evaluated as a fine-tuned backbone for retrieval, code and classification.
- [[MoE-LLaVA and MoE-Tuning]] — MoE-LLaVA turns a vision-language model sparse in three stages, copying the feed-forward layer into experts in the last stage; a 3.6B-activated model beats LLaVA-1.5-7B on some benchmarks and POPE against 13B (self-reported).
- [[Monitoring Layers and What Each Can See]] — Operations, inputs, predictions and outcomes each catch a different failure; a drift score can fire loudly on a harmless input shift and read zero on an expensive new fraud type, so a random audit is what measures cost, and training on reviewed cases alone destroys automation.
- [[Multi-Agent Evolve Self-Play]] — Multi-Agent Evolve trains one model in three roles (proposer, solver, judge) with reinforcement learning on unlabelled seed questions; quality filtering and partial reference use were the stabilisers, on a single 3B model.
- [[Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning]] — MARL, the non-stationarity problem it exists to solve, CTDE as the dominant answer, the value-decomposition and central-critic algorithm families, and the five challenges — including the ~10–20 agent scalability wall.
- [[Multilevel vs Fixed Effects vs Clustered Standard Errors]] — Pick by question: clustered SEs when groups are a nuisance, fixed effects (or a within-between model) when the group effect may correlate with the predictor, multilevel when group-level estimates are the question; plain random intercepts can be biased where the others are not.
- [[Multiple Comparisons Corrections]] — Running many tests makes a false positive likely, 64 percent for 20 tests at 5 percent, so adjust: Bonferroni and Holm control the chance of any false positive, Benjamini-Hochberg controls the share of false discoveries and keeps more power.
- [[MVCC Row Versions and Snapshots]] — Multi-version concurrency control keeps old row versions so readers never block writers; a held-open snapshot pins versions and bloats tables, and table formats repeat the design at file scale.
- [[MVoT Multimodal Visualization of Thought]] — MVoT interleaves generated images with text steps during reasoning (Anole-7B with a token discrepancy loss), scoring 92.95, 95.14 and 85.60 on Maze, MiniBehavior and FrozenLake (self-reported), but plain CoT does better on the two easier tasks.
- [[Named Entity Recognition and Sequence Tagging]] — NER is sequence labelling with BIO tags; a CRF learns tag-transition grammar, closed lists beat models via rules, and evaluation must be entity-level because O dominates tokens.
- [[Native Sparse Attention]] — Native Sparse Attention (NSA) pretrains a model with three gated attention branches (compressed blocks, top-n selected blocks, sliding window) and Triton kernels, reporting 9.0x forward and 6.0x backward speedup over Triton FlashAttention-2 at 64k with quality at or above full attention on a 27B MoE (self-reported).
- [[Neural Probabilistic Language Model]] — Bengio et al. (2003) introduced the idea of learning distributed word representations (embeddings) jointly with a neural network language model — fighting the curse of dimensionality by mapping words to a continuous vector space where semantically similar words have nearby representations.
- [[Neural Text Models vs TF-IDF]] — On 5,000 labelled reviews a TF-IDF bigram logistic regression (0.886) beat averaged embeddings (0.847), a text CNN (0.741) and a BiLSTM (0.716), because from-scratch embeddings overfit; truncation and sequence-loss choices also move scores, and generation and masked losses have their own traps.
- [[Neural Turing Machine]] — A controller network coupled to a memory matrix through differentiable read and write heads, using content-based and location-based addressing, trained by gradient descent to learn copy, recall and sort; on small synthetic tasks it generalised to longer inputs where an LSTM did not (self-reported).
- [[Newsvendor Critical Ratio]] — For a one-off order under uncertain demand, order the quantile of demand equal to underage cost over underage plus overage cost, not the mean; the gain is real but small and forecast error matters more than the rule.
- [[No-U-Turn Sampler (NUTS)]] — NUTS extends Hamiltonian Monte Carlo so the number of leapfrog steps is chosen per iteration by doubling a trajectory until it turns back, and dual averaging tunes the step size during burn-in.
- [[Notebook Dependency Staleness]] — Migration maps for the three library breaks that strand old ML notebooks — sklearn 0.20→1.4, TensorFlow 1.x→2.x, PyMC3→PyMC 5 — plus the two-phase triage that distinguishes a mechanical import swap from a genuine rewrite.
- [[Object Detection Anchors, Non-Max Suppression and Focal Loss]] — Detectors predict a grid of anchor boxes, then filter by score and non-max suppression; one-stage detectors drown in easy background anchors, which focal loss (1-p_t)^gamma discounts, provided the classifier bias starts every anchor at 1% object probability.
- [[Object Store Guarantees and the Partial-Write Problem]] — An object store gives atomic single-key writes and little else: no rename, no multi-key atomicity, so a half-finished overwrite shows readers a wrong answer without error.
- [[OEC, Guardrails and North Star Metrics]] — Experiment metrics split into data-quality checks, one pre-agreed overall evaluation criterion, guardrails tested for non-inferiority against a margin, and diagnostics; a correct test on the wrong metric gives a confident wrong answer, and not significant is not a guardrail pass.
- [[Offline Recurrence Sleep for Language Models]] — Before evicting old tokens from the KV cache, a hybrid state-space/attention model runs several offline recurrent passes over them to update its fast weights, so later answers need one forward pass; the benefit shows on synthetic reasoning tasks at small scale and is a latency shift, not a compute saving (self-reported).
- [[OLS Influence and Collinearity Diagnostics]] — One high-leverage point can flip a regression slope, flagged by leverage and Cook's distance, while near-duplicate regressors inflate standard errors without hurting prediction, flagged by the variance inflation factor.
- [[Olympiad Proof Agents and Verifier Loops]] — Two 2025-26 pipelines reach olympiad-level proofs by wrapping a model in a checker: a solver-verifier-corrector loop on natural-language proofs (5 of 6 IMO 2025 problems), and a Lean agent whose compiler plus a decomposition reviewer gates each step (Putnam 2025 12 of 12).
- [[On-Policy Distillation]] — The student generates its own trajectories and a teacher scores every token by its full next-token distribution (minimise KL) — RL's on-policy data with SFT's dense signal, matching RL on math in about a tenth of the steps; self-distillation (OPSD) gets the same signal from the model itself given privileged context.
- [[One Big Table versus Star Schema]] — A pre-joined wide table reads somewhat faster and costs more storage, but a one-label change rewrites every affected row or file and a second grain has nowhere to go; treat it as a rebuildable cache of a star, never the source of truth.
- [[Open NLP Questions Large Language Models Do Not Solve]] — A 2023 position paper from one academic lab lists 14 research areas with 45 directions it says LLMs do not directly solve, chosen to be thesis-sized and independent of paid APIs; it runs no experiments and is best used as a framing citation.
- [[Open POI Data Sources]] — Measured comparison of open point-of-interest sources for a city venue catalogue — OSM tags, Wikidata, Wikivoyage listings, Foursquare OS Places and Overture Maps — covering licence, channel, per-field fill rate, freshness and bias, with the conclusion that open POI data is a triage and proposal signal behind a human gate, never a write path.
- [[Open-Domain Chatbot Recipes (BST and Minimum-Length Beam)]] — A human-evaluation study of 90M to 9.4B Seq2Seq chatbots finds that skill-focused fine-tuning and a 20-token minimum-length beam matter more than perplexity, and that Wikipedia retrieval did not help engagingness.
- [[Open-Domain Dialogue Systems]] — Survey of frameworks for open-domain conversation — retrieval-based (score candidates), generation-based (seq2seq/PLM), and hybrid methods — with two key goals (informative via knowledge grounding, controllable via persona/strategy/safety).
- [[OpenCUA and AgentNet]] — OpenCUA is an open recipe for desktop computer-use agents built on AgentNet, a 22,625-task human-annotated trajectory set, with reflective long chain-of-thought synthesized per step; the 72B model reaches 45.0% on OSWorld-Verified at 100 steps (self-reported).
- [[Optimal Matching and Journey Sequences]] — Optimal matching measures how alike two customer journeys are as the cheapest edits turning one state string into another; clustering that distance describes journey shapes after the fact but cannot value or predict, and costs are a modelling choice.
- [[Orchestrator Core Loop, Logical Dates and Retries]] — Every orchestrator is a loop that orders tasks by dependency, skips what depends on a failure, retries with backoff and records runs against a logical date that is not the clock; scheduling is the set of expected runs minus the successful ones.
- [[Orchestrator Design Rules and Tool Choice]] — Keep domain logic in a plain package with orchestrator code as thin glue, hand off between tasks through storage, adopt an orchestrator on day 0, and pick the tool the organisation already runs unless starting fresh.
- [[Outcome vs Process Reward Models]] — ORM scores the whole rollout, PRM each step; 2025 practice swung back to ORM plus verifiable rewards, with a light PRM for verification
- [[Overthinking and Concise Reasoning]] — Longer reasoning is not better reasoning: o1-like models spend thousands of extra tokens on easy questions after a correct first answer, and several methods (self-training, length caps, token-complexity thresholds) trade a small accuracy loss for large token savings.
- [[P-Value Misreadings]] — A p-value is the chance of data at least this extreme if the null were true, not the chance the null is true, not an effect size, and not evidence of no effect when large; with millions of users a negligible lift is highly significant.
- [[Pandas Copy-on-Write and Chained Assignment]] — Since pandas 3.0 every indexing or method result behaves as a copy, so chained assignment does nothing and one .loc call is the only way to update part of a frame.
- [[Pandas Index Design, MultiIndex and Reindex]] — Making a real key the index turns lookups into plain label access, but non-unique indexes change the return type and MultiIndex slicing needs sorting; reindex fixes the row list of a report.
- [[Pandas Label Alignment and Dtypes]] — pandas matches values by label rather than position and ties behaviour to per-column dtypes, so NaN promotion and text-typed numbers are the first things to check on any frame.
- [[Pandas loc, iloc and the Integer Index Trap]] — loc selects by label and iloc by position; they agree only until a filter or sort leaves gaps in the default integer labels, after which the same-looking code returns different rows.
- [[Pandas Merge Validation and Many-to-Many Blow-up]] — A pandas merge can silently drop or multiply rows; state how, set validate, then compare row counts and one total before and after every join.
- [[Pandas Missing Data Decisions]] — Pandas can count, fill and drop gaps but cannot say what a gap means; the treatment follows from why the value is missing.
- [[Panel Data Fixed Effects]] — Fixed effects remove all stable differences between units by using only within-unit variation, turning a pooled estimate of -1.0 into the true 0.5, but they absorb time-invariant variables and do not remove time-varying confounders.
- [[Parallel Run and Reconcile at the Grain People Read]] — Moving a warehouse follows expand-migrate-contract at larger scale: run both side by side, compare per partition and key, because totals can match to the cent while no day matches.
- [[Parquet Row Groups, Pruning and Sort Order]] — How Parquet's footer statistics enable column pruning and predicate pushdown, why pushdown only works when physical sort order matches the filter, and how Parquet (compressed, at rest) differs from Arrow (uncompressed, in memory).
- [[Partial Pooling and Multilevel Models]] — A multilevel model estimates every group together so each borrows strength in proportion to how little data it has (weight = tau^2 / (tau^2 + sigma^2/n)); it beats both no pooling and complete pooling in simulation, but boundary estimates and few groups make the variance parts fragile.
- [[Partial Pooling for Segment Winners]] — When an experiment reports a lift per segment, the raw winner is overstated; pooling segment estimates removed most of the exaggeration in simulation (+2.93 to +0.09) but picked the best segment only modestly better, and it fails for outliers, wrong centres and when the prior on tau is wrong.
- [[Partition Pruning, Clustering and Column Pruning]] — Layout decides what an analytical query reads: named columns, partition filters and sort order each cut bytes scanned, and the savings multiply.
- [[Partitioning Strategies and Hot Spots]] — Range versus hash versus compound partition keys, why hot keys need salting, how secondary indexes fan out, and why rebalancing needs many fixed partitions rather than hash mod N.
- [[Passive Retraining and the Promotion Gate]] — Retrain on a schedule, score champion and challenger on the same holdout newer than both, and move the alias only on a win beyond a margin; the gate decides what proceeds to rollout, not what users get.
- [[Past-Tense Refusal Jailbreak]] — Rewriting a harmful request in the past tense bypasses refusal training on several frontier models, and fine-tuning on past-tense refusals closes the gap at a cost in overrefusal.
- [[PCA, NMF and Low-Rank Factorisation]] — PCA rotates to maximum-variance axes, NMF builds additive non-negative parts, collaborative filtering factorises a mostly missing matrix; scale before PCA.
- [[PDDL-INSTRUCT Logical-CoT Planning]] — PDDL-INSTRUCT tunes an LLM to write symbolic plans step by step, restating preconditions, effects and the full state after every action, with an external plan validator supplying feedback during training; Llama-3-8B reaches 94% valid plans on Blocksworld (self-reported, three PlanBench domains).
- [[Peeking and Sequential Testing]] — Checking a fixed-horizon p-value repeatedly inflates false positives to 25% at 20 looks, so if people will look use group-sequential boundaries (O'Brien-Fleming, Pocock) or always-valid mSPRT p-values, which pay for looks with a stricter threshold.
- [[Peeking Under Bayesian Stopping Rules]] — Bayesian tests are not immune to peeking: a daily P(B > A) > 0.95 rule shipped 23.8% of no-effect changes in simulation, and an expected-loss rule ships quickly but needs a minimum sample size to stop harmful launches.
- [[PEGASUS Gap-Sentence Pretraining]] — PEGASUS pre-trains an encoder-decoder by masking the whole sentences that score highest in ROUGE1-F1 against the rest of the document and generating them as a pseudo-summary; the 568M model beat BART on XSum ROUGE-2 and fine-tuned from 1,000 examples beat the previous best on 6 of 12 datasets (self-reported).
- [[Penalised Linear Models: Ridge, Lasso and Elastic Net]] — L2 shrinks all weights, L1 zeros some, Elastic Net mixes them; alpha raises simplicity while classifier C works the opposite way.
- [[Per-Group Calibration Offsets by Partial Pooling]] — When groups share one score but differ in base rate, correct each group with one shrunken log-odds offset: a per-group threshold on few labels pays everything when none are positive, one global threshold ignores real differences.
- [[Per-User Metrics Depend on Window Length]] — Any metric with the user as its unit grows with the observation window because users get more chances to return, so comparing periods of unequal length manufactures a change; match windows or use sessions.
- [[Permutation Test]] — A permutation test builds the null distribution by shuffling group labels, needing few assumptions; classical t-tests are approximations to it and agree closely on well-behaved data.
- [[PGD Adversarial Training]] — Adversarial robustness cast as a min-max problem: the inner step attacks each input with multi-step projected gradient descent and the outer step trains on those worst cases; the paper reports robust MNIST and partly robust CIFAR10 classifiers (self-reported), for images only.
- [[Pipelines Prevent Preprocessing Leakage]] — Any fitted preprocessing step must be refit inside each CV fold; a Pipeline does that, and feature selection outside the loop turns pure noise into a strong score.
- [[Platt Scaling for SVM Probabilistic Outputs]] — Platt scaling fits a two-parameter sigmoid P(y=1|f)=1/(1+exp(Af+B)) by maximum likelihood on held-out SVM outputs, with smoothed targets, so a sparse SVM yields calibrated posteriors; in 1999 tests it made fewer errors than a raw SVM on some tasks and was neither better nor worse than a regularized-likelihood machine overall (self-reported).
- [[Point-in-Time As-Of Join for Features]] — A feature row has a window it describes and a moment it became knowable; joining a training row to the latest feature known at its event time, not the same-hour window, keeps the offline metric honest even though it makes it look worse.
- [[Point-in-Time Recovery from Base Backup and WAL]] — A base backup plus every archived WAL segment recovers a database to a chosen moment just before a bad delete, on a new timeline, which a nightly dump and a replica cannot do.
- [[Poisson Offsets and Overdispersion]] — Model rates by adding log exposure as an offset in a log-link count model, then check dispersion: when variance exceeds the mean the Poisson standard error is too small by a factor of three and robust or negative binomial errors are needed.
- [[Population Stability Index]] — PSI compares a score's current distribution with the one a model was built on, bin by bin, needing no outcomes, so it warns of drift years before defaults arrive; its 0.10 and 0.25 thresholds are conventions.
- [[Position Interpolation]] — Position Interpolation (PI) extends a RoPE model's context window by scaling position indices down into the trained range instead of extrapolating past it, and recovers a working 8K to 32K window from LLaMA after about 1,000 fine-tuning steps (self-reported).
- [[Positional Encoding]] — Sinusoidal or learned position signals injected into Transformer input embeddings — required because self-attention is permutation-invariant and has no inherent notion of sequence order.
- [[Post-Training for Agents]] — How SFT, RL and distillation divide the work of training an agent — SFT writes form and protocol, RL learns transferable strategy, on-policy distillation gives RL's on-policy data with SFT's dense signal — and why the environment, data and reward shape matter more than the choice of algorithm.
- [[Post-Training Reasoning Data Primer]] — A 2026 survey of over 150 public studies on the data side of reasoning post-training: difficulty is relative to the base model, small curated sets work only inside the base model's support, coverage is a recipe, and self-play moves curation rather than removing it; it reports no results of its own.
- [[Post-Treatment Segments and Simpson's Paradox]] — A trend can reverse inside every subgroup of aggregated data; which table to trust depends on the causal story, so segment on traits fixed before treatment, never on something the treatment changes, and in experiments keep the allocation constant across periods.
- [[Posterior Predictive Checks]] — A precise posterior can still be wrong; simulate new datasets from the fitted model and compare them with the real data on a quantity the model was not tuned to match, ideally also reporting the whole probability band rather than a line.
- [[Potential Outcomes and Selection Bias]] — Each unit has two potential outcomes but only one is seen, so a naive treated-minus-untreated comparison equals the effect on the treated plus selection bias; opt-in comparisons mostly measure selection (1.21 against a true 0.30), and controlling for the selection driver recovers it only if nothing unmeasured matters.
- [[Pre-Norm vs Post-Norm and Warm-Up]] — Post-norm keeps large output-layer gradients that need learning-rate warm-up (without it BLEU 8.45 vs about 34; at lr 0.01 accuracy stuck at 0.101), while pre-norm's gradients shrink with depth and train without warm-up.
- [[Precision-Recall vs ROC and Average Precision]] — ROC AUC is a ranking probability unaffected by class balance, while PR curves and average precision track the flagged set on rare positives; the two can disagree and PR points must not be linearly interpolated.
- [[Precomputed Labels, Not Refits on Filter]] — Cluster ids are arbitrary and refits on filtered data move customers between groups, so a batch job should publish labels and the app should only read them.
- [[Preference Optimization Algorithms]] — The PPO → DPO → GRPO → KTO/IPO/ORPO family — what each removes from the stage before it, the five-algorithm decision table, and why the field shifted from choosing an algorithm to designing a reward structure.
- [[Prior Choice and Prior Predictive Checks]] — A wide prior is not uninformative once a model transforms it; simulate outcomes from the prior before seeing data, pick weakly informative priors on a standardised scale, and remember priors wash out with data but a zero-mass region never does.
- [[Prior Shift Correction for Oversampled Defaults]] — When bad outcomes are oversampled, a model's probabilities describe a population that does not exist; adding a constant to the log odds fixes the level without changing ranking, so Gini and KS cannot reveal the error, and the approve/decline cutoff must come from costs on the corrected probability.
- [[Probabilistic Relevance Framework (BM25 and BM25F)]] — The survey that derives BM25 and BM25F from the probability ranking principle through eliteness and saturation, and gives starting ranges and a tuning order for k1 and b.
- [[Probability Calibration and Reliability Curves]] — A score is calibrated when a 0.8 happens 80% of the time; check with a reliability diagram and Brier score, and fix on held-out data with isotonic, Platt or temperature scaling.
- [[Profile Then Select]] — ml-transform profiles every column with stated thresholds and never applies; ml-select refuses to pick before the profile exists, parks unknowns with triggers, emits a draft when nobody can answer
- [[Programming Curriculum]] — Course map for the three languages an AI or data engineer writes daily — Python, SQL, TypeScript — with the phase order, the hand-offs between tracks, the rules of thumb each track ends on, and where each topic's wiki page lives.
- [[Pseudonymised Is Not Anonymised: Hashing Attacks and Keyed Pseudonyms]] — Hashing a low-entropy identifier is pseudonymisation at best; a keyed hash with the key outside the lake, scoped per domain, is the minimum.
- [[Pseudoreplication and Intraclass Correlation]] — Rows from the same unit are correlated, so m rows per unit with intraclass correlation rho inflate the variance of a mean by 1+(m-1)rho; analysing at a finer level than the randomisation unit makes standard errors too small and null effects look significant.
- [[Python Closures and Decorators]] — Functions are values: closures capture variables (not values) from their defining scope, a decorator is a function that wraps a function (always with `functools.wraps`), and a decorator with arguments adds one more level — the shape behind timing, retry and caching wrappers.
- [[Python Concurrency Models]] — Pick by what you're waiting on: threads (ThreadPoolExecutor) for a few dozen blocking I/O calls, asyncio for hundreds of concurrent requests or async SDKs, processes for CPU-bound pure Python — and in asyncio, never block the event loop, bound fan-out with a Semaphore, add deadlines, and retry only transient errors with backoff plus jitter.
- [[Python Data Model and Idioms]] — Names are labels on objects, not boxes — which explains aliasing, the mutable-default trap and pass-by-assignment; dunder methods map syntax onto your classes; hashability rules decide what can be a key; and a short list of idioms (unpacking, EAFP, dict merge) should be reflexive.
- [[Python Exceptions and Context Managers]] — Catch only what you can handle, chain re-raised errors with `raise … from err`, give each package one exception base class with fields callers can branch on, and put cleanup in `with`/`finally` — `@contextmanager` turns a try/finally around a `yield` into a reusable resource guard.
- [[Python Foundations Reference]] — Compact Python-for-Everybody-level reference — types and operators with their traps (floor division, truthiness, short-circuit guards), return-don't-print functions, loop patterns and their built-ins, strings and files, collections and aliasing, regex, HTTP, JSON/XML APIs, classes and sqlite3 — plus the course's rules of thumb.
- [[Python Generators and Lazy Iteration]] — Iterators are single-use and generators run only on demand, so chained generator pipelines process arbitrarily large inputs in constant memory — but a second pass over an exhausted iterator silently yields nothing, and a generator's open `with` block lives until it is consumed.
- [[Python Packaging and Tooling]] — A modern Python project is a `pyproject.toml`, a `src/` layout and `tests/`, run with uv (envs, deps, lockfile), ruff (lint + format) and pytest (plain asserts, parametrize, fixtures) — and most import bugs come from `sys.path` and stdlib-shadowing filenames, fixed by installing the project rather than editing paths.
- [[Python Type Hints and Dataclasses]] — Type hints are checked by tools, not Python: type every signature (loose in, specific out, `X | None` to force None handling, `object` over `Any`), use Protocols for checker-understood duck typing, frozen dataclasses for trusted internal data, and Pydantic to validate untrusted data at every boundary.
- [[PyTorch Training Loop Silent Bugs]] — A checklist of training-loop mistakes that raise no error but cost accuracy: softmax before a cross-entropy that already applies it, forgetting zero_grad, confusing eval() with no_grad(), the dropout p convention, and distributed-sampler details.
- [[Query-Only Test-Time Training (qTTT)]] — qTTT caches the key/value states of a long prompt and then takes a few gradient steps on the query projection alone, on the model's own context, to counter score dilution in softmax attention; with Qwen3-4B it beats a FLOP-matched thinking budget on LongBench-v2 and ZeroScrolls (self-reported).
- [[Queue Staffing with Little's Law and Erlang C]] — Little's law (L = lambda W) validates a queue log exactly and gives waits from a head count; Erlang C describes steady state but a shift that starts empty runs below it; price the extra worker and solve rosters as a linear programme.
- [[Quorums, Version Vectors and Last-Write-Wins]] — Leaderless replication reads and writes several replicas with w + r > n so sets overlap; concurrent writes are resolved by version vectors that keep siblings, or by last-write-wins that silently drops acknowledged writes.
- [[Random Forests and Bagging]] — Averaging deep trees cuts variance only as far as their errors are decorrelated, which is why forests subsample features at each split; the out-of-bag score is a free validation estimate.
- [[Ranking by Lower Credible Bound]] — Sort items with few observations by a low percentile of each item's Beta posterior instead of the raw ratio, so 1 upvote with 0 downvotes cannot outrank 999 and 1; the same fix applies to star ratings and to LLM prompts compared on eval pass rate.
- [[Ranking Metrics as the Confusion Table Along a List]] — Precision and recall at k are the confusion table with k as the threshold; MRR and NDCG summarise across cut-offs and can disagree, so pick by whether one good answer or several are needed.
- [[Rare-Class Benchmark Quotas and Reweighting]] — A production-mix benchmark learns almost nothing about a rare class; equal quotas fix the spread but bias the headline unless each class's accuracy is reweighted back to its production share.
- [[Rate and Mix Decomposition]] — Split a change in any aggregate into a rate (within-segment) effect and a mix effect by holding one fixed at a time; the split depends on order, so report it roughly, and it decomposes a change without proving cause.
- [[Ratio Metrics and the Denominator Trap]] — When treatment can change a ratio's denominator, the ratio can fall while the numerator and the business improve; never report a ratio without its parts, choose ratio of averages or average of ratios deliberately, and take the standard error with the user as the independent unit.
- [[Ratio of Sums vs Mean of Rates]] — A group's rate is the sum of numerators over the sum of denominators; averaging per-row rates weights tiny and huge rows equally and can reverse the ranking of groups.
- [[Re-embedding a Vector Store with a Second Index]] — A new embedding model is a schema change the database cannot see: queries still run and return garbage, so build a second collection, dual write, shadow test on retrieval, then swap an alias and keep the old one for rollback.
- [[Real-Time Multimodal Agents]] — Voice dialogue, computer use and robot control are the three settings where continuous input and a strict output time budget make multimodality hard — and all three are moving the same way, from serial pipelines toward end-to-end models and a fast/slow division of labour, with irreversible actions needing a gate.
- [[Reconciliation Against a Producer Control Total]] — Nothing inside a table can show it is true; the cheapest accuracy test compares it with a second, independent account such as the producer's own daily row count, and it fails if that count is computed after a retry.
- [[Recurrent Memory Transformer (RMT)]] — RMT passes a few memory tokens from one fixed-size segment to the next on a pretrained transformer, giving linear compute and constant memory; it reached 2M tokens on synthetic fact tasks, though memory is lossy and the tasks are easy.
- [[Reflection in Pre-Training]] — A study of OLMo-2 checkpoints reports that self-correction behaviour already appears during plain pre-training and grows with pre-training compute, measured by injecting errors into chains of thought and appending 'Wait,'; it argues against reflection being a product of RL alone.
- [[Regression Covariate Adjustment]] — OLS on a treatment dummy reproduces the t-test, and adding pre-assignment covariates shrinks the standard error without biasing a randomised estimate, while adding post-treatment or omitting confounders in observational data changes the answer entirely.
- [[Regression Discontinuity]] — Units just either side of a cutoff are nearly comparable, so fitting a line on each side and reading the jump estimates the effect at the cutoff; report several bandwidths and check for pile-up at the cutoff, and do not extrapolate away from it.
- [[Regularisation as a Prior]] — Ridge is the MAP estimate under a Normal prior and lasso the MAP under a Laplace prior, with penalty strength equal to prior precision; lasso's exact zeros come from the MAP's geometry, not from a Bayesian belief in sparsity.
- [[Regularisation Knobs and Early Stopping]] — Every family has a complexity-limiting knob whose validation curve peaks in the middle; early stopping is the same penalty in training time, but libraries may keep the rounds waited past the best.
- [[Regularisation Under Noisy Labels]] — With 30% flipped labels a wide net memorises (train 1.000, clean test 0.694); early stopping on a clean validation set beat weight decay and dropout, and dropout should be off at test time and, for underfitting models, only used early.
- [[Reinforcement Learning Foundations]] — The MDP tuple (S, A, P, R, γ), the Markov property that makes RL tractable, the four algorithm families, and the exploration/exploitation tension — with how each maps onto LLM training and agentic tool use.
- [[Reinforcement-Learned Teachers]] — Train a small model with RL to write explanations for known question-solution pairs, rewarded by how well a student can reproduce the solution from them; in the paper a 7B teacher's raw traces gave better student models than traces distilled from much larger reasoners.
- [[Rejection Sampling Fine-Tuning]] — Sample several candidates per prompt, keep only those a rule, test or reference answer verifies, deduplicate and cap per prompt, SFT on the survivors, and repeat as the model improves — the STaR/RFT loop that sits between SFT and RL with no reward model and no policy gradient.
- [[Relational, Document and Graph Models and Schema on Read]] — Choosing a data model by the relationships the data has: documents suit self-contained trees, relational suits shared and many-to-many facts, graphs suit variable-length paths; plus schema-on-read versus schema-on-write and why declarative queries free the engine.
- [[Rendering Many Points: WebGL, Datashader and Bundle Weight]] — Measured costs of drawing many points (SVG vs WebGL vs server-side datashader), shipping chart libraries, and mapping; ask first whether the points are needed at all.
- [[Replica Lag and Replication Slots Retain WAL]] — Reads from a standby are stale even on an idle pair, long standby queries get cancelled by replay conflicts, an unread replication slot holds WAL until the disk fills, and capacity forecasts must include operations that need a table's size again.
- [[Replication Lag, Failover and Read Anomalies]] — Leader-based replication, why logical row logs power change data capture, how asynchronous replication loses acknowledged writes and reuses IDs on failover, and the read anomalies lag produces on replica-fed dashboards.
- [[Replication, Backups and the Durability Ladder Above the Disk]] — A replica survives the machine but copies your mistakes in milliseconds; async replication loses the lag at failover, sync replication stalls commits when the standby stalls, and only a restored backup with archived WAL survives a bad deploy.
- [[Repo Weight Triage — GC Before LFS]] — measure .git layers first; gc, dedupe, untrack media, then gitignore+manifest; against submodules and LFS at personal scale
- [[Rerunnable Cleaning Loader with Assertions]] — Keep the raw file untouched and put every cleaning step in one function that asserts what must be true and prints row counts, so the next export runs through the same checks.
- [[Resampling Before the Split Leaks]] — Resampling then cross-validating puts copies or interpolations of training rows into validation folds, turning a true 0.70 AUC into 0.98; keep the sampler inside the pipeline so it runs only on training folds.
- [[Residual Connections and the Degradation Problem]] — A block that outputs F(x)+x makes the identity the easy default, fixing the optimisation failure of very deep plain networks (62-layer plain net: train 0.379), and makes blocks optional so stochastic depth can drop them as regularisation.
- [[ReST Meets ReAct Agent Self-Improvement]] — Recipe that runs a prompted multi-step search agent, ranks its trajectories with an LLM, and fine-tunes on them for two iterations using AI feedback only; reported to bring PaLM 2 XS and S close to the prompted L teacher (self-reported).
- [[Restore Drill with an Invariant]] — Only a restore proves a backup: schedule the restore on separate hardware, check a data invariant rather than an exit code, record the time taken, and alert on missing success; a replica is not a backup.
- [[Revenue Bridge Query]] — A revenue bridge labels each customer-month new, returning or reactivated with a lag window over a CTE chain, so a flat-looking total splits into parts that move in opposite directions; the labelling gap is a stated choice and the total must reconcile to source.
- [[Reverse ETL Fingerprint Sync]] — Copy modelled data into a downstream tool by upserting on a stable ID, sending only records whose fingerprint changed, and recording each send only after it succeeds, so a crash costs nothing and a rerun sends only the rest.
- [[Review Route Thresholds From Priced Costs]] — Adding an ask-a-person route turns one threshold into two set by where three expected-cost lines cross; it beats a single threshold by a wide margin, but depends on the reviewer's cost and catch rate and on queue capacity.
- [[Reward Hacking and Overoptimization]] — The three failure modes of optimizing against a learned reward proxy — reward hacking, the inverse-U overoptimization curve against KL distance, and the ~15% alignment tax on academic NLP benchmarks.
- [[RFM Segmentation Validated on a Holdout]] — RFM segments are predictions and must be scored on a period after the snapshot; doing so shows that frequent buyers who went silent are mostly gone (iso-value curves bend backwards), that clustering adds little over R and F, and that value is concentrated but only partly predictable.
- [[RISE Recursive Introspection]] — Fine-tune one model on multi-turn rollouts where it sees its earlier wrong answers and revises them, so that sequential revision beats parallel sampling on math; in the paper, prompted self-refine lowered accuracy and plain single-turn SFT on the same data did not teach it.
- [[RL Training Frameworks for LLMs]] — verl vs OpenRLHF vs TRL by scale ceiling (70B+ / ~30B / ~13B) and algorithm support, with a selection matrix
- [[RLHF Pipeline]] — The three-stage InstructGPT pipeline — SFT, then a Bradley-Terry reward model on ~33k comparisons, then PPO with a KL penalty against the SFT policy — and why all three stages are load-bearing.
- [[RLMF Metacognitive Advantage Scaling]] — Reinforcement learning with metacognitive feedback scales each GRPO completion's advantage by how accurately the model judged its own confidence faithfulness, instead of adding a reward term; on faithful calibration of small models it beat plain GRPO in the paper's runs.
- [[RLVP — Verifiable Path Penalties]] — Add deterministic per-action penalties (and matching compliant-action bonuses) to an outcome reward, R = O + β·Φ, so agent RL learns outcome-neutral constraints like 'don't edit the tests' and recovers the within-group variance GRPO needs — with four design rules that avoid the inaction trap.
- [[RMSE vs Top-N Ranking in Recommenders]] — A recommender is judged on the short list it shows, not on rating error; the model with the best RMSE lost to a popularity list on top-10, so score full-catalogue ranking, split by time, and put popularity and simple linear models in every table.
- [[RoboCat Self-Improving Robot Generalist]] — RoboCat trains one vision-based goal-conditioned policy across robot arms and tasks, fine-tunes it on a few hundred demonstrations, lets it collect its own data and retrains; the appendix evidence shows self-generated data is mostly failures and matches the data's success rate (self-reported).
- [[Robust Standard Errors]] — When residual spread changes with the fitted value, classical OLS standard errors are wrong while the coefficients stay fine; HC3 heteroskedasticity-robust errors fix the standard error, not the estimate.
- [[Robust Summaries for Skewed Metrics]] — When mean and median disagree, the tail is running the summary: choose location, spread and correlation measures the shape allows, and report percentiles and the mean-to-median ratio instead of one number.
- [[Role Anchor and Role Drift]] — Role drift is the loss of a module's assigned job when a compound LLM pipeline is trained end to end with a final-answer reward; the Role Anchor penalty on a role-utility measure kept a RAG reader evidence-following (0.869 against 0.589) in a small study (self-reported).
- [[Rolling Out a Decision Router: Partial Shadow and an Audit-Sized Canary]] — A router that pays, reviews or rejects can shadow only where the champion produced outcomes, so it needs a canary with a stable unit, action limits and a clock set by label arrival, with an audit sized to the error jump it must detect.
- [[Row Stores vs Column Stores]] — OLTP and OLAP pull storage in opposite directions: row stores win on key lookups, column stores win on scans by about two orders of magnitude through pruning, compression, vectorisation and block skipping; includes rollups and the column-store write path.
- [[RuleTaker]] — Clark et al. fine-tune RoBERTa-large on synthetic natural-language facts and rules and report it answers true/false questions at about 99% on its own test data, generalises to deeper proofs, and transfers to paraphrased rules, while stopping short of proving it reasons (self-reported).
- [[Runtime Validation with Zod]] — Types are erased, so env vars, JSON bodies, files, tool inputs and LLM output are really `unknown`: parse them once at the boundary with a zod schema that yields both the runtime check and the static type (`z.infer`), then trust the typed value everywhere after — parse, don't validate.
- [[RWKV Linear-Time Recurrent Language Model]] — RWKV replaces query-key attention with a channel-wise time-decay operator that runs in parallel for training and as a fixed-size-state recurrence for decoding; the paper reports parity with similar-sized transformers from 169M to 14B parameters.
- [[Safe DDL Recipes for Defaults, NOT NULL and Indexes]] — Split the slow proof from the strong lock: constant defaults avoid a rewrite, NOT NULL goes through NOT VALID then VALIDATE, and indexes use CONCURRENTLY, which leaves an INVALID index if it fails.
- [[Safety Stock, EOQ and Service Levels]] — EOQ balances order and holding cost on a flat cost curve; safety stock is z times the spread of lead-time demand including lead-time variance, and a flat reorder point misses its service target in seasons where demand runs higher.
- [[Salesforce Koa]] — Koa is Salesforce's Nemotron-3-Super-120B model trained with a GRPO variant in spec-driven CRM environments; it scored 87% on the internal CRMAgentBench against 82% for GPT-4.1, while giving back 2.3 BFCL points after the in-domain stage (all self-reported, single runs).
- [[Same-Weekday Median and MAD Volume Monitor]] — A row-count baseline built from the median and MAD of the last six values for the same weekday catches a 25% loss that a 28-day mean-and-three-sigma band misses almost every time; validate any threshold by injecting losses into real history.
- [[Sample Ratio Mismatch]] — Compare arm counts with the designed split using a chi-square test before reading any outcome; a failure means users were lost or added non-randomly, the test is stopped rather than reweighted, and A/A tests validate the whole pipeline.
- [[Sampled Ranking Metrics and MRR]] — Ranking each positive against 99 sampled negatives is a different metric from the full ranking, inflates scores several-fold and flipped the order of two models; rank the whole catalogue, and use MRR only when one good result ends the task.
- [[SayCan Affordance-Grounded Skill Selection]] — SayCan picks a robot's next skill by multiplying a language model's score for the skill text by a learned value-function affordance for the current scene; with PaLM 540B on 101 instructions it planned correctly 84% of the time and executed 74% in a mock kitchen (self-reported).
- [[Scaling Laws for Precision]] — Kumar et al. fit loss as a function of parameters, data and numeric precision: post-training quantization hurts more as data grows, compute-optimal training precision is about 7-8 bits, and weights lose effective parameters below roughly 6-7 bits.
- [[Scheduled Sampling]] — A training schedule for recurrent sequence generators that mixes the true previous token and the model's own sampled token as decoder input, with the true-token probability decaying over training, to narrow the gap between training and decoding.
- [[Selective Risk Guarantees: SGR and Conformal Sets]] — When a contract names an error rate instead of a price, a binomial-bound threshold search (SGR) keeps the promise but needs many labels; conformal sets are not a good cost router, yet class-conditional ones bound missed positives on any scorer.
- [[Self-Attention Mechanism]] — Self-attention (intra-attention) relates different positions of a single sequence to compute a representation — the core primitive enabling Transformers to model long-range dependencies in O(1) path length.
- [[Semantic Analysis, WordNet and Lexical Ambiguity]] — WordNet organises senses into synsets with is-a and part-of links, Lesk disambiguates by definition overlap and fails without it, and logic-based semantics evaluates assertions.
- [[Semantic Layer: Metrics Compiled at the Requested Grain]] — A semantic layer stores metrics and join keys as data and compiles each request, refusing fan-out joins, recomputing ratios from parts and handling balances as semi-additive.
- [[Semantic Turn Detection]] — Replacing VAD's silence threshold with streaming perception that judges end-of-turn from meaning plus acoustics — why VAD + segmented ASR fails on latency, information and accuracy, the two streaming paths, and the label-leakage bug where the fix was causal re-labelling, not a new architecture.
- [[Sensitivity Analysis for Unmeasured Confounding]] — E-values and Rosenbaum bounds ask what hidden confounder would erase an effect entirely and so can look reassuring while real bias is smaller; the whiteboard bias approximation gamma times delta (hidden effect per SD times remaining imbalance) sizes the bias, and refuters only catch pipeline errors.
- [[Sentence Embeddings as Features for Classical ML]] — A pretrained sentence encoder turns text into a cached matrix for ordinary scikit-learn heads; it beats TF-IDF most when labels are few, at about 50 times the encoding cost.
- [[Sentiment Analysis Methods and Failure Modes]] — Lexicons reach about 70 percent with no labels, TF-IDF bigrams plus a linear model about 90 percent, and sequence models overfit without more data; a maxlen mismatch at inference silently costs 11 points.
- [[Separated Storage and Compute and Warehouse Billing Shapes]] — Why modern warehouses split storage from compute, how the main engines differ in layout and billing, and why Redshift-style distribution keys decide join cost.
- [[Seq2seq Bottleneck and Attention]] — Encoding a whole input into one vector bottlenecks long inputs; letting the decoder weight all encoder states at each step fixed date normalisation (0.032 to 1.000 whole dates), and removing the recurrence from this idea gives self-attention.
- [[Sequence Models vs Hand-Built Event Windows]] — On generated card events with a planted order-dependent fraud pattern, boosting on velocity windows and recent events beat a GRU (PR-AUC 0.855 vs 0.791); order matters, but hand-written order features may capture it, and untuned baselines hand the GRU undeserved wins.
- [[Sequence-Level Knowledge Distillation]] — Seq-KD trains a small student on the teacher's beam-search outputs instead of its word distributions; Kim and Rush (2016) report students about 10x faster than the teacher with little BLEU loss on English-German translation.
- [[SHAP Attributions and the Limits of Explanations]] — SHAP splits one prediction into a baseline plus per-feature log-odds that sum exactly; it explains the model, not the world, and studies show explanations help only for some tasks and people.
- [[Shifted Beta Geometric Retention Model]] — The sBG gives each customer a constant per-period churn probability drawn from a beta distribution, so aggregate retention rises with age without anyone becoming loyal, and it extrapolates a cohort's survival far better than curve fits.
- [[Shuffle Skew and Salting]] — Joins and group-bys are shuffles; broadcast joins avoid moving the big side, and because hashing cannot split one key, a hot key makes one reducer the straggler, which salting plus a second aggregation stage reduces.
- [[SimCSE Contrastive Sentence Embeddings]] — SimCSE fine-tunes BERT or RoBERTa with an in-batch contrastive loss, using two dropout masks of the same sentence as the unsupervised positive pair and NLI entailment/contradiction pairs when labels exist.
- [[Similar vs Interchangeable in Matching]] — Text similarity rewards both same-part and look-alike pairs, so a matcher's score should propose while extracted attributes decide (clash rejects, all read and agreeing accepts, unread goes to a person); the label chosen is itself a design decision.
- [[SkillGym]] — SkillGym is a set of 2,756 containerised task environments built around human skills, with 8,364 verified teacher trajectories used for SFT; fine-tuning a Qwen3.5-35B-A3B student lifted GDPval-AA, Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SkillsBench scores, and the trained agent without skills beat its skill-assisted base (self-reported).
- [[Slicing Errors With Correction and the Rate Identity]] — Slice error cost by nameable subgroups with Wilson intervals, correct the scan for many tests, confirm on unread rows, and expect group error-rate gaps because FPR, PPV and FNR are tied by an identity.
- [[Slim CI for dbt Models: state:modified and defer]] — Build only what a pull request changed by comparing the project with the production manifest, resolve unselected parents from production with defer, write to a per-PR schema, and make the test check required so a failing test stops the merge.
- [[Slowly Changing Dimensions Types 1, 2 and 3]] — Overwriting a dimension label rewrites published history; type 2 closes the old row and opens a new surrogate key so facts keep the label in force at event time, and a hash compare makes the load safe to re-run.
- [[Small Files, Prefix Layout and Storage Tiers]] — On an object store, file count and size decide request cost and whether column pruning works: partition by what you delete or backfill by, sort by what you filter on, and remember cold storage tiers lose money for tables that are read often or made of small objects.
- [[Small-N Trap and Shrinkage]] — Averages from small groups are noisy, so the best and worst groups of any league table are the smallest ones even when no group differs; show N beside every rate, rank by a lower bound, or shrink toward the overall rate with pseudo-observations.
- [[Spaced Repetition and Self-Quizzing From a Knowledge Base]] — Retrieving material beats rereading it at a delay but not immediately, and the best gap between sessions grows with how long you need to keep it; a KB suits recall questions asked and graded later, with no credit for how a quiz feels straight after reading.
- [[Spark Shuffles and When One Machine Is Enough]] — What a groupBy and a join cost in a distributed engine, which Spark habits to avoid, and when DuckDB or Polars on one machine is the better answer.
- [[SQL Date Spine and Window Frames]] — Grouping drops periods with no rows, which inflates averages and lies on charts; start from a table of every period and left join. Also: explicit window frames, lag-by-rows pitfalls, QUALIFY for top-N and a one-scan funnel with FILTER.
- [[SQL Fan-Out and Source Reconciliation]] — Joining before aggregating repeats rows of the one side once per match and inflates sums with no error; aggregate the many side to the one side's grain first, and reconcile every report total to the source.
- [[SQL from Python]] — SQL does the set operations and Python orchestrates: sqlite3 connection/cursor mechanics (the `with` block commits but doesn't close), parameters instead of f-strings with allow-lists for identifiers, DuckDB for SQL over files and DataFrames, and filter-in-SQL/reshape-in-pandas as the division of labour.
- [[SQL Indexes and Query Plans]] — A B-tree index turns a full scan into a log-n lookup but every write pays for it; read EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN for SCAN vs SEARCH, order composite indexes equality-first/range-last, keep predicates sargable (no function on the indexed column), and know when indexes don't help — including columnar engines that use zone maps instead.
- [[SQL Join Patterns]] — Choosing inner, left, self, anti and cross joins by what should happen to unmatched rows — and the two silent-wrong-answer traps: a WHERE filter on a LEFT JOIN's right table turning it back into an inner join, and fan-out when the join key isn't unique.
- [[SQL NULL and Three-Valued Logic]] — NULL means unknown, so any comparison with it is unknown and WHERE drops it — which silently breaks `!=` filters, `= NULL`, and `NOT IN` over a nullable subquery; plus the related expression traps: half-open date ranges, integer division, and dialect-specific string/date functions.
- [[SQL Query Fundamentals]] — Core SELECT/WHERE/ORDER BY/CASE/LIMIT and GROUP BY/HAVING mechanics, organised around the logical evaluation order (FROM → WHERE → GROUP BY → HAVING → SELECT → ORDER BY → LIMIT) that explains most alias and filtering errors, plus the rules of thumb that prevent silent wrong answers.
- [[SQL Schema Design and DDL]] — A schema makes promises about its data and constraints let the database keep them: the four data-hygiene rules behind normal forms, PRIMARY KEY/UNIQUE/CHECK/FOREIGN KEY semantics (and SQLite's off-by-default foreign keys), safe UPDATE/DELETE, ON CONFLICT upserts for idempotent loads, atomic transactions, and views vs materialised views.
- [[SQL Sessionisation and Gaps-and-Islands]] — Two window tricks for grouping consecutive rows without loops: a running sum of gap flags numbers sessions, and a running index minus row_number is constant along an unbroken run so it serves as an island key.
- [[SQL Subqueries and CTEs]] — A query is a table, so it can go anywhere SQL expects a table or value: scalar, correlated and derived-table subqueries, EXISTS vs IN (and why NOT IN breaks on NULL), CTEs for readable multi-step queries, and recursive CTEs for hierarchies and gap-free date series.
- [[SQL Window Functions]] — A window function computes over a group but keeps every row — the tool for top-N per group, dedup-latest, running totals, moving averages, lag/lead comparisons and share-of-total; it runs after GROUP BY so it must be filtered from an outer CTE, and its default RANGE frame silently groups ties.
- [[Staggered Rollout Bias in Two-Way Fixed Effects]] — With staggered adoption and effects that grow, one TWFE coefficient can be badly wrong because already-treated units serve as controls; a clean comparison against never-treated units recovers the effect.
- [[Staging Dedupe Keys and Fact Join Row Loss]] — A dedupe is only as right as its key, and an inner join in a fact silently turns missing dimension rows into missing revenue with every test green; left join through an explicit unknown row and reconcile.
- [[Standardised Survival and Pricing an Intervention]] — Turn a fitted hazard model into two survival curves (everyone treated, nobody treated) averaged over the real customer mix, so the gap becomes months gained and money; valid only if the observed covariates explain who got treated.
- [[Statistical Test Selection]] — Pick the test from the data type and design: Welch by default for two means, paired when rows pair, chi-square or Fisher for counts by expected cell size, rank tests for ordinal data but not for questions about totals.
- [[Stored Instruction Computer LLM Memory Universality]] — Schuurmans shows a frozen LLM plus an external associative memory and a regex loop can run a universal Turing machine, verified by 29 greedy prompts on Flan-U-PaLM 540B; it is a computability argument, not evidence about how agents use memory.
- [[Straight-Through Estimator]] — The straight-through estimator trains hard-threshold or stochastic binary gates by backpropagating through the threshold as if it were the identity; Bengio and colleagues (2013) tested it against three other estimators on a small gated-layer experiment.
- [[Stratified and Cluster Survey Design]] — Stratify to guarantee coverage of known groups, allocate proportionally unless one statistic dominates (Neyman allocation helps one number and hurts others), and cluster only when travel cost beats the design-effect loss.
- [[Stream Joins Replay and Lambda Architecture]] — Stream joins depend on cross-stream event order, so a replay can silently change history unless the joined table is versioned; and a stream is worth its cost only if a faster number changes a decision, otherwise a micro-batch or one replayable stream path beats lambda.
- [[Subliminal Learning]] — A student model fine-tuned on a teacher's outputs on unrelated data can pick up the teacher's traits, including misalignment, when both start from the same initialization, and filtering the data does not reliably prevent it.
- [[Supervised Model Families and Complexity Knobs]] — Each classical supervised family has one knob that sets its complexity, a typical strength and a typical failure; sweep the knob first and start with a simple model.
- [[Survey Error Sources and Nonresponse Bias]] — A margin of error measures only sampling error from a probability sample; coverage, nonresponse and measurement errors are larger and do not shrink with more replies, and nonresponse bias comes from the link between answering and the answer, not the response rate.
- [[Survey Weighting, Raking and Effective Sample Size]] — Weighting respondents to known population totals (post-stratification or raking) removes only bias carried by the auxiliary variables; it inflates variance by Kish's 1 + L, and the margin must be multiplied by deft and still ignores leftover bias.
- [[Survival Analysis for Customer Lifetimes]] — Customer lifetimes are censored, so averaging them is biased; Kaplan-Meier, Cox hazard ratios, time-varying covariates, competing risks and a person-period logit each handle one trap, and a single hazard ratio can hide an effect that wears off.
- [[Symbol Tuning]] — Finetuning on in-context input-label pairs whose labels are replaced by arbitrary symbols and whose instructions are removed, so the model must infer the task from exemplars; helps most when prompts carry no meaningful labels.
- [[Synthetic Computers at Scale]] — A pipeline that turns personas into synthetic computers with files, collaborators and a month of simulated work, then distils skills from run retrospectives; the skills lifted a held-out score from 61.6% to 68.6% (self-reported) with no weight updates.
- [[Synthetic Control Method]] — With one treated unit and many untreated donors, fit non-negative weights summing to one on the pre-period so the weighted donors track the treated unit, then read the post-launch gap; inference comes from placebo runs, not conventional standard errors.
- [[t-SNE and UMAP Visualisation Rules]] — t-SNE preserves neighbourhoods for 2-D pictures only; read cluster shape not size or distance, vary perplexity, and never feed it to a model.
- [[Table Format as a Commit Log over Parquet]] — A table format turns a bucket of immutable Parquet files into a table by keeping an ordered log of live files, committed by one atomic create, which gives atomic commits, time travel and optimistic concurrency.
- [[Tabular ML Leakage Taxonomy]] — Six leakage classes for tabular ML — target, temporal, group, fit-before-split, resampling outside the fold, train/serve skew — all answering one question ("at the moment this line runs, what does it know that it would not know at prediction time?"), plus why a leakage verdict needs the person who knows how the data was collected, not a correlation ranking.
- [[Testing an ML System Beyond Its Code]] — Code tests pass on a correct function fed broken data, so also test data validity, model behaviour (minimum functionality, invariance, direction) and model sanity (overfit a tiny batch), with injected seams and a container integration test against fake cloud services.
- [[Text Classification Metrics: Macro-F1, Log-Loss and Calibration]] — Which metric fits which text task: macro-F1 exposes ignored small classes, log-loss punishes overconfident Naive Bayes, and multi-label needs micro versus macro averages and per-label thresholds.
- [[Text Preprocessing and the Stop-Word Negation Trap]] — Each text-cleaning step decides what a later model can see; default stop-word lists delete negations and flip sentiment, and the order of steps matters.
- [[Thompson Sampling and Bandit Regret]] — Thompson sampling draws one sample from each arm's posterior and pulls the highest, so exploration is automatic and fades with evidence; regret measures the cost, and which rule wins depends on the gaps and horizon, so simulate your own.
- [[Three Ways to Be Serializable]] — Serial execution, two-phase locking and serializable snapshot isolation each buy serializability at a different price: a throughput ceiling, blocking and deadlocks, or aborts under contention.
- [[Three-Level Duplicate Classification]] — Multi-source entity resolution as not/possible/confirmed duplicate from deterministic, entity-type-specific signals; the uncertain middle goes to review, confirmed duplicates hide via the existing gate, nothing auto-merges
- [[Tidy Data and Long-Wide Reshaping]] — Ask what one row means; tidy data puts each variable in a column and each observation in a row, and melt, pivot, stack and unstack move between long and wide layouts.
- [[Time-Series Leakage: Shift and Rolling Windows]] — shift and rolling windows build comparisons and features from the past, but a negative shift or a centred window reads the future and leaks, and count-based windows misbehave on irregular rows.
- [[Time-Series Resampling, Bins and Gaps]] — Absent rows hide gaps in time data, and resample is a time-bin groupby whose closed and label edges, unequal period lengths and fill rules change the numbers.
- [[Tool-N1 Binary-Reward Tool-Calling RL]] — Tool-N1 trains Qwen2.5 models for tool calling with GRPO and a binary reward on output format plus exact call match, with no reasoning-trace supervision; its 7B and 14B models edge past GPT-4o on single-turn BFCL (self-reported), and pure RL matches SFT-then-RL by a hair.
- [[Toolformer Self-Supervised Tool Use]] — Fine-tuning recipe in which a model samples API calls into plain text, executes them and keeps only those that lower the loss on following tokens; GPT-J 6.7B so trained is reported to beat GPT-3 175B on several zero-shot tasks (self-reported).
- [[Topic Models and Extractive Summarisation]] — LSI, LDA and NMF factor the document-term matrix into readable topics; choose k by coherence plus reading, check seed stability; document clustering and TextRank/LSA summaries share the same machinery.
- [[Train, Validation and Test Discipline]] — The test set is touched once; tuning happens on validation or CV folds, and every score is read against the majority-class base rate.
- [[Train-Serving Skew and the Shared Feature Function]] — Skew is the same input producing different features in two code paths; an unknown category vectorises to all zeros without an error, so the fix is one normalising feature function, a contract test, and logged serving features.
- [[Training-Agent Disaggregation]] — Make an existing agent trainable without rewriting it: trace runs as state/action/reward into an external trainer, bind trainable roles to logical model names a proxy re-routes, and train each role with the algorithm that fits (SFT, PPO, contextual bandit).
- [[Transaction Isolation Levels and Anomalies]] — What ACID letters actually promise and which anomaly each isolation level still allows: read skew, lost updates, write skew and phantoms, with the fixes (atomic update, row locks, constraints, serializable) and why level names differ across engines.
- [[Transfer and Multi-Task Learning Conditions]] — Transfer learning needs a much larger source task and shared low-level features, and can lose to training from scratch (frozen backbone 39.48% vs scratch 11.82% error); multi-task learning with masked loss uses partially labelled rows and halved error versus keeping only complete rows.
- [[Transformer Architecture]] — The Transformer model architecture (Vaswani et al., 2017) — encoder-decoder stacks of self-attention and feed-forward layers that replaced RNNs/CNNs for sequence transduction, enabling parallelized training and constant-length dependency paths.
- [[Transformer Encoder Silent Shape Bugs]] — Three transformer-encoder bugs raise no error and still train: attending to padding (or an inverted mask), the batch_first=False default that makes digits attend across the batch (0.966 as a batch, 0.658 one at a time), and a (batch,1) vs (batch) broadcast in the loss.
- [[Tree Ensemble Interpretation Beyond Impurity Importance]] — Impurity importance rewards overfit noise; permutation importance on held-out data fixes that but is fooled by correlated columns, and partial dependence needs ICE lines and describes the model, not causes.
- [[Tuning an Embedding Model from Match Labels]] — A frozen pretrained encoder scored below a character n-gram baseline on code-heavy records; about 1,000 labelled pairs with in-batch softmax lifted recall@10 from 0.134 to 0.908, but a held-out product family dropped it to 0.715, so measure frozen, tuned and held-out against a character baseline.
- [[Two-Tower Retrieval with logQ Correction]] — A two-tower model embeds users and items separately so item vectors precompute into an ANN index and new items embed from features; in-batch softmax over-penalises popular items unless log sampling probability is subtracted in training only.
- [[TypeScript any Escapes]] — The three ways out of an `any` — a real type, `unknown` with narrowing, or a generic — plus why types files are the highest-leverage place to fix them and how exhaustive-deps catches stale closures rather than style violations.
- [[TypeScript Async Patterns]] — A JS promise starts running when it is created (unlike an asyncio coroutine), so the choice is sequential vs Promise.all parallel; Promise.race does not cancel the loser, forEach does not await, and an unbounded Promise.all over thousands of requests needs a concurrency limit — pass an AbortSignal down to cancel real work.
- [[TypeScript Discriminated Unions and Narrowing]] — Model 'one of these' as a union with a literal `kind` field, narrow by control flow, and end the `switch` with `const x: never = s` so adding a member without handling it is a compile error; type predicates package a check but are trusted, not verified.
- [[TypeScript for AI Apps]] — Treat an LLM API as an untyped, slow, occasionally failing remote call: narrow content blocks by `type` (never assume `content[0]` is text), drive the tool loop from an exhaustive `stop_reason` switch, stream long requests, validate tool inputs and structured output with zod, and keep parsing and loop logic pure so it tests offline.
- [[TypeScript for Python Developers]] — TypeScript is JavaScript with a checker that runs and then disappears, so learn the runtime traps first (`==` coercion, string-comparing `sort()`, truthy `[]`, reference equality), then annotate boundaries, prefer `unknown` over `any`, and turn on `strict` from day one.
- [[TypeScript Generics and Type Transformations]] — Generics keep the caller's type through a function (a type parameter should appear at least twice); keyof/T[K], overloads, utility, mapped, conditional and template-literal types derive new types from one source — and type-level code is real code, so stop before infer goes three levels deep.
- [[TypeScript Modules and Tooling]] — Most 'TypeScript is hard' pain is module and config pain: use ESM with named exports and explicit `import type`, one strict tsconfig (nodenext, verbatimModuleSyntax, erasableSyntaxOnly, noEmit), run with Node type stripping and check with tsc separately, and use .d.ts files and module augmentation for untyped or extended libraries.
- [[UI-TARS Native GUI Agent]] — UI-TARS trains one vision-language model on screenshots to emit thoughts and keyboard-mouse actions, replacing prompted agent frameworks; the 72B DPO model reports 24.6 on OSWorld at 50 steps against 22.0 for Claude Computer Use (self-reported).
- [[UL2 Mixture of Denoisers]] — UL2 pre-trains one model on a mixture of three denoising objectives (regular, sequential, extreme) tagged with mode tokens, arguing the objective matters more than encoder-decoder versus decoder-only; the paper reports gains over T5 and GPT-like baselines and a 20B model (self-reported).
- [[Unlimiformer]] — Unlimiformer replaces cross-attention in a pretrained encoder-decoder with a kNN lookup over the encoder states of the whole input, so one index serves every head and layer; it improves summarization at book length, and does nothing when the input is already ranked.
- [[Unreliable Networks, Clocks and Process Pauses]] — A timeout means 'unknown', not 'failed'; clock skew of a few milliseconds makes last-write-wins drop acknowledged writes and inverts cause and effect; processes can pause unnoticed; checksums catch corruption that still parses.
- [[Vacuum, Bloat and Idle Transactions]] — Updates leave dead row versions that VACUUM only frees for reuse, VACUUM FULL returns space under an exclusive lock, and one forgotten idle transaction stops cleanup entirely; transaction-ID wraparound is the failure to know by name.
- [[Validating Clusters Without Labels]] — Internal indices, stability and classifier accuracy all look good on data with no groups, so compare every one against a shuffled-column control and validate on outcomes from outside the data.
- [[Variational Inference and the ELBO]] — Variational inference replaces an intractable posterior with a simple q chosen by maximising the ELBO (reconstruction minus KL to the prior), whose gap to the log evidence equals the KL to the true posterior; it scales well but mean-field versions understate variance under correlation.
- [[VERA Co-Evolving Model and Harness]] — VERA alternates RL on the model with verified edits to its harness, using thousands of environments with rubric stage rewards; at 9B the full loop beat either axis alone, and at 27B it came within a point or two of frontier models on the benchmarks' default runners (self-reported).
- [[Verbalized Probability Calibration and CalibratedMath]] — Finetuning GPT-3 to append a stated confidence, as a number or a word, to its arithmetic answers, tested for calibration under distribution shift on a 21-task suite.
- [[Vision-Language Model Training Families]] — Most VLMs are built by connecting a pretrained vision encoder to a pretrained LLM and training only the connector (a linear map, an MLP or a Q-Former), which is cheap but inherits the LLM's hallucinations and biases; resolution, encoder capacity and visual data matter more than how the two are joined.
- [[Visual Encoding Accuracy Ranking]] — Readers judge some visual channels far more accurately than others, so put the comparison that matters on the most accurate channel and choose the chart from the question.
- [[Voice Agent Paradigms]] — Cascaded (VAD → ASR → LLM → TTS), end-to-end omnimodal, and full-duplex voice agents differ in one thing — how far each gets from VAD's assumption that conversation happens in discrete turns — and all three coexist as trade-offs; the cascade's serial latency budget is about 1–2 s before queueing multiplies it.
- [[Warehouse Cost Meters: Bytes, Slots and Egress]] — A warehouse bill is bytes scanned or slot-hours for compute, a small storage line and route-dependent egress; compute the on-demand versus capacity break-even before choosing, using list prices that carry a read date.
- [[Warehouse, Fabric and Lakehouse: Sizing the Physical Architecture]] — Modern data warehouse, data fabric and lakehouse differ in how many copies and governance layers they add; size the choice by measuring one query, and name the system of record.
- [[WaveNet — Autoregressive Audio Generation]] — WaveNet (van den Oord et al., 2016) is a deep autoregressive generative model for raw audio waveforms using dilated causal convolutions — achieved state-of-the-art TTS naturalness (MOS >4.0) and demonstrated multi-speaker conditioning, music generation, and speech recognition from raw audio.
- [[Weekly Seasonality Forecasting Methods]] — Weekly data has a non-integer yearly period (about 52.18 weeks) and moving holidays; STL+ETS, dynamic harmonic regression and Prophet handle it differently, and most of their edge comes from knowing the calendar.
