- [[MemInsight Attribute Augmentation]] — MemInsight has an LLM annotate each memory with attribute-value pairs (ordered by relevance) and retrieves through those attributes; its LoCoMo recall at k=5 is 60.5 against 26.5 for dense retrieval, but answer F1 is only marginally better and the paper's headline percentages do not match its tables (self-reported).
- [[MeMo Memory as a Model]] — MeMo fine-tunes a small memory model on question-answer 'reflections' generated across a document corpus and lets a frozen LLM query it; it beats retrieval baselines on two of three benchmarks and ties on the third, at hundreds of GPU-hours of training per corpus (self-reported).
- [[Memo, Not Memory: Frozen State Position]] — A position paper argues that context-only agent memory leaves the generator's adaptive state unchanged, so it is a memo rather than learning, supports this with a conditional Omega(k^2) lower bound on stored examples, and proposes a hybrid of deletable retrieval plus audited asynchronous consolidation; it has no new experiments.
- [[Memory Architecture]] — Three-tier cognitive memory model (semantic/episodic/procedural), SQLite implementation pattern, context window management strategies, and self-improving reflection pattern for assistant agents.
- [[Memory Curse Expanded Recall]] — A 2026 study of repeated social games finds that giving LLM agents a longer window of their own interaction history lowers cooperation in 18 of 28 model-and-game settings by the paper's own threshold; the extraction's caveats show the effect is noisy and the 'cursed' label is applied loosely.
- [[Memory Intelligence Agent]] — MIA, a memory framework for deep-research agents, stores distilled workflow summaries for a Planner instead of feeding raw memory to the Executor; its ablations show raw memory alone slightly hurts while memory for the Planner helps, and raw-context memory methods score below no memory in its tables (self-reported).
- [[Memory Transfer Learning in Coding Agents]] — A 2026 study pools memories from other coding benchmarks and finds that the more abstract the memory format (Insight over Summary, Workflow, Trajectory), the better it transfers across domains; gains are 1 to 8 points per benchmark on small samples (self-reported), and trajectory memory can anchor the agent to an incompatible fix.
- [[Memory-R1 RL Memory Manager]] — Memory-R1 trains a Memory Manager that picks ADD, UPDATE, DELETE or NOOP using only downstream answer exact match as reward, plus an Answer Agent that distills retrieved memories; F1 45.02 against 35.04 for the strongest baseline on LoCoMo (self-reported).
- [[MemoryBank Long-Term Memory]] — MemoryBank stores timestamped dialogs with hierarchical event and personality summaries, retrieves them with a dual-tower encoder over FAISS, and updates them with an Ebbinghaus-style decay; its evaluation uses simulated users and never ablates the forgetting rule.
- [[MemWalker Summary-Tree Navigation]] — MemWalker builds a tree of recursive summaries over a long document and has the LLM navigate it by prompting, reverting to the parent when a branch is wrong; it beat recurrence, retrieval and truncation on long inputs with a 70B model and a 4K window (self-reported).
- [[MSCE Memory-to-Skills Governance]] — MSCE promotes procedural policies from layered memory into a skill library only with evidence from repeated episodes and a positive gain, and gates skill use by a reliability score; on EvoAgentBench its ablations rank flat retrieval worst and skill crystallization the most consistent component (self-reported, single run, vendor authors).
- [[Multi-Session Agent Benchmarks]] — MemoryArena (701 tasks whose later sessions depend on earlier ones) and OdysseyBench (602 office tasks whose instructions are scattered across multi-day dialogue) both find that agents do poorly when earlier sessions must be used, and that retrieval or summaries do not reliably beat plain long context (self-reported).
- [[OCR-Memory Optical Context Retrieval]] — OCR-Memory renders an agent's history as images, has a trained retriever pick segment indices, and returns the stored verbatim text; on Mind2Web and AppWorld it beats text baselines at a 4096-token memory budget and cuts injected text tokens 6.7x, at the price of more disk and latency (self-reported).
- [[PAHF Pre- and Post-Action Feedback Personalization]] — PAHF personalises an agent with a per-user note memory fed by two feedback channels, a clarifying question before acting and a correction after, so it can both learn a preference and notice when it changes; in simulation the combined version led after drift (self-reported).
- [[RAISE Conversational Agent Memory]] — ReAct loop extended with a dialogue-level scratchpad and turn-level retrieved examples, fine-tuned on 848 real-estate scenes; the paper's gain over ReAct plus examples is within noise under GPT-4 prompting (self-reported).
- [[Recuris Experiential and Working Memory]] — Recuris pairs a small distilled skill library with a verified working-state tracker; on tau2-Retail the working state carried the gain, injecting the whole library every turn scored below no skills, and test-time adaptation was mostly explained by the retry budget (self-reported).
- [[Self-Controlled Memory Framework]] — Memory stream plus a prompted controller that decides when to retrieve, ranks by recency and relevance and chooses full text or summary; reported on a 105-question dialogue probe, where removing activation memory collapses accuracy (self-reported).
- [[Self-Learning Agents]] — Four-level improvement stack for production agents — inference-time (ReAct, CoT, self-critique), session-time (reflection, procedural memory), operational (learning loop, HITL), and training-time (DPO). Most agents need the first three; DPO is a late-stage investment.
- [[Memory Lifecycle]] — Five stages — represent, store, retrieve, use, update/forget — with the fifth being the one production systems skip, and consolidation designed so a failed merge can roll back instead of losing history.
- [[Memory Decay Weighting]] — Exponential recency decay as a retrieval scorer — Memoria weights memories by e^(-alpha*age), which resolves stale-vs-current facts by ranking rather than by an explicit conflict-resolution step.
- [[Memory Forms Taxonomy]] — The Forms/Functions/Dynamics survey framing — token, parametric, or latent substrate; factual, experiential, or working purpose — and why almost all agent memory work occupies one cell of a much larger space.
- [[Memory Store Operations]] — Running a memory store in production — caching and async indexing for latency, memory hit rate as the metric that says whether memory is earning its cost, and the deletion requirement that makes memory a compliance surface.
- [[Memory-Augmented Conversational RAG]] — Multi-turn retrieval breaks because the query is under-specified — the fixes are query rewriting against history, a when-to-retrieve policy, and asking a clarifying question instead of retrieving on an ambiguous turn.
- [[Identity Memory: Revision vs Accretion]] — what frameworks offer for a changing self-model (Letta persona block, Mem0 consolidation, Zep bi-temporal invalidation) and what a prose-file identity system learned: record dispositions, gate pre-commit, enforce by hook
- [[MemGPT / Letta Virtual Context Memory]] — LLM-as-OS memory: the agent pages facts between an in-context core block and archival/recall stores via its own tool calls
- [[Multi-Writer Agent Memory Systems Comparison]] — Letta/Mem0/Cognee/Zep/layered memory compared for shared team stores; scoping keys are not auth
- [[Designed Memory vs Raw-Record Search]] — Extraction-based memory (fact stores, temporal graphs) decides what to keep before any question arrives; a 2026 study found it beat full context only with the weakest model, that a weak writer caps every stronger reader, and that a strong agent searching the verbatim history did best at under half full context's cost — directional evidence from one conversation.
- [[Shared Epistemic Memory]] — A persistent key-value store that agents share, where every entry carries a timestamp, a writing-agent id and an expiry, so reads can weigh provenance and old facts expire; a design sketch from a pattern book.
- [[Stateless Decision Memory (DPM)]] — DPM keeps an immutable event log and builds the agent's memory as a projection at decision time instead of maintaining a running summary; in one small study it beat incremental summarization only when the memory budget was tight (self-reported), and it shrinks the audit surface to two calls.
- [[Zero-Mem Zero-Token Memory Operations]] — Zero-Mem keeps raw conversation traces and builds its retrieval structure with encoders, entity tagging, BM25 and graph propagation instead of LLM calls; it reports higher LoCoMo and long-context HotpotQA scores than every LLM-memory baseline at zero memory-operation tokens (self-reported, single runs).

## MCP

- [[A2A Agent Protocol]] — Google's Agent-to-Agent open specification for inter-agent communication — task lifecycle, agent cards, and how it maps to LangGraph primitives.
- [[Agent Communication Protocol Taxonomy]] — Sander et al. classify nine open-source agent protocols (MCP, A2A, LAP, agents.json, Agora, ANP, LMOS, ACP, agntcy) on five dimensions and forecast a layered stack; the work is qualitative, hand-classified, and its tables contradict parts of its text.
- [[Agent Interoperability Protocol Stack]] — The five open protocols standardizing agent integration — MCP, A2A, A2UI, AP2, UCP — partitioned by what sits on the other end of the boundary: data, agent, human, or money.
- [[Alita]] — Alita is a generalist agent with one web agent and no predefined toolkit: it writes, runs and stores its own tool scripts as 'MCP' packages; it reports 75.15% pass@1 and 87.27% pass@3 on GAIA validation (self-reported, best-of-k against single-run baselines).
- [[MCP Protocol]] — Model Context Protocol — how it separates tool definitions from agents, enabling independent deployment and runtime tool discovery; includes AWS Bedrock AgentCore deployment pattern from the Hypernova PoC.
- [[MCP Server Architecture Patterns]] — A 2026 industry paper sorts 15 MCP servers into five architecture patterns and reports from one voice-AI deployment that tool-selection accuracy dips below 90% between 10 and 15 tools for Haiku 4.5 and at 30 for Sonnet 4 (self-reported, observational).
- [[MCP Server Security Patterns]] — Security patterns for MCP servers — read-only invariant, sandbox isolation, secrets handling, and what to never expose over MCP.
- [[Tool Design as Context Engineering]] — Tools consume context twice — definitions sit in the window permanently, results enter per call — so token-efficient results, unambiguous boundaries, and terse routing descriptions are context decisions, not API aesthetics.
- [[Curl-Bridge Context Injection]] — A proxy injects instructions to curl allowlisted read-only endpoints with server-side identity overwrite — memory access for any shell-capable agent without MCP.
- [[Provenance-Tracked Agent Beliefs]] — Design proposal: A2A beliefs carry source and contradictions, PeerModels track what peers know, AttentionBrief is the default message
- [[REST-First, MCP as Adapter]] — versioned REST is the service boundary; MCP servers are thin adapters with no business logic
- [[A2UI Protocol]] — declarative agent-to-UI JSON: flat adjacency list, JSON-Pointer data binding, v0.8 vs v0.9, blank-screen failure ladder
- [[Tool Poisoning and Protocol-Level Attacks on Agents]] — Two 2025 surveys describe attacks that ride the agent-to-tool and agent-to-agent channel: poisoned tool descriptions, tampered tools, retrieved-document injection, chained tool calls and loop exhaustion; one survey shows five single-run demos and Ferrag et al. collect second-hand attack rates.
- [[TURA Tool-Augmented Unified Retrieval Agent]] — TURA routes a query to MCP servers using an index enriched with synthetic queries, decomposes it into a DAG of tool calls and runs a distilled Qwen3-4B executor; on MCP-Bench Recall@5 rose from 0.4187 to 0.8289 (self-reported).

## Evaluation

- [[2WikiMultiHopQA]] — A 192,606-question multi-hop QA set generated from Wikipedia text and Wikidata triples, with the triples as evidence paths; the paper reports a single-hop model scoring 8.7 F1 lower than on HotpotQA and baselines far below humans (self-reported).
- [[Agent Plasticity]] — A metric for self-improving agents defined as held-out score gain per unit learning cost; across board-game tasks one model had the highest fitted endpoint while another had the highest plasticity (298 points per $1,000 for GPT-5.6 Sol versus 57 for Claude Fable 5, self-reported), so endpoint score and learning efficiency can rank models differently.
- [[AgentBench]] — A 2023 benchmark of eight interactive environments for LLMs as agents, scoring 29 models at temperature 0; gpt-4 had the top overall score and Task Limit Exceeded was the most common failure cause (self-reported).
- [[Agents' Last Exam (ALE)]] — ALE is a living benchmark of 1,490 expert-contributed computer tasks in 13 domains (150 public) graded by code against hidden reference outputs; the best configuration, Codex with GPT-5.5, passed 24.0% of public tasks and none of the hardest tier, with partial-credit scores far above pass rates (self-reported).
- [[AGIEval]] — A benchmark of 8,062 objective questions from 20 human exams (Gaokao, SAT, law, LSAT, civil service, GRE/GMAT, AMC/AIME) in English and Chinese; GPT-4 averaged 58.4 under zero-shot chain of thought against a human average of 67 (self-reported), but the human figures are not measured on the same items.
- [[ALCE Citation Benchmark]] — ALCE asks a prompted LLM to retrieve, answer and cite from 100-word passages on ASQA, QAMPARI and ELI5, scoring fluency (MAUVE), correctness and NLI-checked citation recall and precision; the paper reports weak citation quality even for ChatGPT and GPT-4 (self-reported).
- [[ALCUNA New Knowledge Benchmark]] — ALCUNA is a benchmark of questions about artificial organisms that cannot be in any training set, testing how well LLMs use new in-context knowledge; multi-hop association with existing knowledge is hardest for all four models tested (self-reported).
- [[ALE-Bench]] — Sakana AI's benchmark of 40 AtCoder Heuristic Contest optimisation problems where an agent's score is placed on the human leaderboard and converted to an Elo-like performance; o4-mini-high with 4-hour iterative refinement reached rating 2104 (top 11.8%) but only 1520 average performance (self-reported).
- [[AlpacaFarm Simulated Human Feedback]] — AlpacaFarm replaces crowdworker pairwise labels with a pool of prompted LLM annotators and scores systems by win-rate against Davinci003 on 805 instructions; across 11 systems the paper reports that method rankings match those from 10k real human preference pairs (self-reported).
- [[AlphaEval]] — A benchmark of 94 tasks taken from 7 companies that deploy agents commercially, scoring whole agent products rather than bare models; the best configuration (Claude Code with Opus 4.6) scored 64.41 of 100, the same model ranged from 53.45 to 64.41 across four scaffolds, and a 27-company survey found 63.0% had low confidence that a model update improved their product (self-reported).
- [[API-Bank]] — Executable tool-use benchmark of 73 APIs and 314 human-annotated dialogues at three levels (Call, Retrieve+Call, Plan+Retrieve+Call), with a Lynx-7B baseline fine-tuned on ChatGPT-agent-generated data (self-reported).
- [[ARC-AGI-3]] — ARC-AGI-3 is an interactive benchmark of turn-based grid environments with no stated goal, scored by RHAE, the squared ratio of a human action count to the agent's; on its semi-private set the best frontier model scored 0.50% (Opus 4.6), while the authors say every environment was solved by at least 2 of 10 humans (self-reported).
- [[Arena-Hard-Auto and BenchBuilder]] — BenchBuilder filters crowdsourced Chatbot Arena prompts by seven LLM-scored quality criteria and topic clusters into a 500-prompt benchmark judged pairwise by GPT-4-Turbo; the authors report 3x the separation of MT-Bench and 98.6% confidence agreement with human rankings at about $20 per model (self-reported).
- [[ARES Automated RAG Evaluation]] — ARES fine-tunes small judges on synthetic data for context relevance, answer faithfulness and answer relevance, then uses prediction-powered inference with a small human set to score RAG systems with confidence intervals.
- [[ASQA]] — A long-form QA dataset of 6,316 ambiguous factoid questions whose answers must cover every interpretation, scored by DR, the geometric mean of ROUGE-L and a reader-based Disambig-F1; 2022 T5-large baselines sit far below humans (self-reported).
- [[AutoLab]] — A benchmark of 36 long-horizon optimisation tasks run for 2 to 12 hours each; the authors report that persistence in benchmarking, editing and using feedback predicts score better than first-attempt quality, with several models quitting early, and that the harness moved scores as much as the model did (self-reported, correlational).
- [[bAbI Toy Tasks]] — Twenty automatically generated question-answering tasks, each isolating one reasoning skill, with a 95% pass threshold; Weston et al. report an extended Memory Network averaging well above LSTM (49%) and N-gram (34%) baselines but failing path finding (self-reported).
- [[BABILong Benchmark]] — BABILong hides bAbI reasoning facts inside PG19 book text padded to any length; on it GPT-4-Turbo failed for most of its 128K window and retrieval failed on multi-fact tasks, while a small recurrent-memory GPT-2 held up to 10M tokens on the simple tasks tested (self-reported).
- [[BARTScore]] — BARTScore rates generated text by the log-probability BART assigns to producing it from the source (or the reference from it), with the direction chosen per quality aspect; the authors report beating existing metrics in 16 of 22 settings, with prompts helping semantic overlap but not factuality (self-reported).
- [[BBQ Bias Benchmark]] — BBQ is 58,492 hand-written three-choice questions probing nine social bias categories in ambiguous and disambiguated contexts; the authors found QA models fell back on stereotypes when the context was under-informative, with bias scores that cannot certify a model unbiased (self-reported).
- [[Benchmark Prompt Format Sensitivity]] — The same model on the same benchmark can move by more than 20 accuracy points, and swap ranks with its rivals, when only the prompt template, answer format or scoring choice changes; report the exact prompt, keep per-item outputs and never mix numbers from different harnesses.
- [[Best-of-k Headline Inflation]] — Agent benchmarks often headline the best of several runs, which can sit far above what one run delivers: ScienceAgentBench's o1 scores 42.2% best-of-3 but 27.1% averaged over runs, and MLE-bench's medal rate doubles from pass@1 to pass@8; check which number is reported, and whether an agent could pick its best run on its own.
- [[BIG-bench]] — A community-built benchmark of 204 tasks from 450 authors at 132 institutions, run on models from 2M to 175B parameters; the best models scored under 20 on the normalised aggregate where expert raters would score close to 100, and about 5% of tasks showed sudden gains with scale (self-reported).
- [[BigToM Model-Generated Benchmark]] — BigToM is a theory-of-mind benchmark of 5,000 items that GPT-4 writes by filling the variables of a causal template, with code assembling 25 controlled conditions per template; crowd raters scored the items above crowd-written ones and slightly above expert-written ones, and GPT-4 was near ceiling on forward belief but far below humans on backward belief (self-reported).
- [[ChatEval Multi-Agent Debate Judges]] — ChatEval has several LLM judges with distinct personas read each other's replies over a fixed number of turns and then vote or average; on FairEval's 80 questions the gain over a single judge was 6.2 points for ChatGPT and 2.5 for GPT-4, and only with diverse roles.
- [[ChatGPT Information Extraction Evaluation]] — A 2023 preprint scored ChatGPT zero-shot on seven information-extraction tasks across 14 datasets, finding it far below supervised models except on entity typing, and its verbalised confidence badly calibrated while its self-judged explanation quality sat near 100%.
- [[ChatGPT Zero-Shot NLP and Annotation Studies]] — Two 2023 ChatGPT studies: a 20-dataset zero-shot survey finding strong NLI and sentiment scores but weak NER and summarisation against fine-tuned models, and a short implicit-hate study where ChatGPT explanations were clearer than human ones but no more informative (both self-reported).
- [[ClashEval Prior Versus Context Benchmark]] — ClashEval is a 1,294-question benchmark that perturbs retrieved context to contradict the model's own answer and measures which side the model follows; its headline says models adopt wrong context over 60% of the time, though a table gives a lower figure for one model (self-reported).
- [[CLI Agent Failure Timeline]] — Zhao et al. model a failed coding-agent run with three hindsight-labelled timestamps (decisive error, lock-in, first visible sign): across 1,184 failed Terminal-Bench runs the median decisive error is at step 7 of a median 27, and 28% of failures never surface (self-reported).
- [[CLUE Chinese Benchmark]] — A GLUE-style benchmark of nine Chinese language understanding tasks plus a 514-pair diagnostic set; the best 2020 baseline averages 74.90 against 85.09 for humans, a gap of about 10 points (self-reported).
- [[Compositionality Gap]] — The share of 2-hop questions a model gets wrong although it answers both sub-questions right: about 40% across GPT-3 sizes on Compositional Celebrities (self-reported), and a related gap for chained GSM8K pairs, measured against the product of the two separate accuracies, was 2-12x worse for cheaper models at similar GSM8K scores (self-reported).
- [[Connect Four AlphaZero Build Task]] — A single-task benchmark in which coding agents build an AlphaZero-style Connect Four player in 3 hours on one consumer GPU and are graded by playing a perfect solver; one agent won as first mover in 7 of 8 trials, and an odd early-stopping pattern in another prompted a small, inconclusive sandbagging probe (self-reported, n = 8 per agent).
- [[ContinualSkillBench]] — ContinualSkillBench runs ordered streams of 100 tasks per domain to test whether agents that maintain SKILL.md libraries beat agents that only keep context and feedback; on Law, Finance and Healthcare the two scored about the same (normalized reward 0.582 against 0.585, self-reported).
- [[Cost-of-Pass and Efficient Agent Design]] — Cost-of-pass divides the expected spend per attempt by the success rate, which lets an agent design be judged on cost per solved task instead of accuracy alone; a GAIA component study and a survey of cost levers both point toward simple memory, bounded steps and selective tool use, with the study's gaps listed in Limits.
- [[Counterfactual Task Variants]] — Test whether a model's success is general by changing the task's world (number base, indexing, axes, chess setup) while keeping the reasoning procedure fixed, with a comprehension check to separate misunderstanding from failure; GPT-4 showed a large drop from base-10 to base-9 addition across 1,000 additions per base (self-reported).
- [[DataSpace]] — A benchmark of 410 data-analytics tasks over mixed-format workspaces, scored by a model-free table comparison; with one backbone held fixed the agent harness moved accuracy by 15.36 points, and most failures of the audited model were wrong output shape rather than wrong analysis (self-reported, one run per configuration).
- [[DS-1000]] — 1000 data science code problems adapted from StackOverflow over seven Python libraries, scored by test cases plus surface-form constraints, with perturbed variants to expose memorisation; Codex-002 scored 43.3% pass@1 and a numpy pilot showed it fall from 72.5% to 23.6% under semantic perturbation (self-reported).
- [[Dynabench]] — Dynabench is an open platform where annotators write examples to fool a live target model, other humans validate them, and models are retrained between rounds, so the benchmark moves as models improve; the 2021 paper reports validated error rates of 33-44% on four tasks (self-reported).
- [[E-Commerce Operation Benchmarks]] — Two 2026 benchmarks that have an agent run an online store for a simulated 365 days and score terminal assets: E-Commerce Bench (18 models, 5 episodes each; top mean 1,431,425 yuan, 10 of 90 episodes bankrupt) and MerchantBench (best LLM configuration 27.3% of the human mean); all numbers self-reported.
- [[ELI5 Long-Form QA Dataset]] — ELI5 pairs 272K Reddit questions with paragraph-length answers and TF-IDF-built web support documents, showing that a multi-task Seq2Seq model still hallucinates and ROUGE cannot rank abstractive against extractive systems.
- [[Embers of Autoregression]] — The behavioural patterns that result from a model being optimised for next-word prediction, such as sensitivity to output probability and task frequency; on a subset of the original tasks OpenAI o1-preview improved but still did better when the output was more probable, and used more thinking tokens on low-probability outputs (self-reported).
- [[ETHICS Benchmark]] — ETHICS is a dataset of over 130,000 scenarios for testing whether a language model predicts everyday moral judgments across justice, deontology, virtue, utilitarianism and commonsense morality; pre-2021 models did poorly, especially on its adversarially filtered Hard Test (self-reported).
- [[Evaluation-Driven Model Selection]] — Huyen's four-step workflow for choosing a model (filter on hard attributes, narrow with public information, run your own evaluation pipeline, monitor in production), why public benchmarks are a filter for bad models rather than a way to find the best one, and the three-step recipe for the pipeline you can trust.
- [[Extract-and-Evaluate Monitoring]] — Split an agent monitor in two: an extractor picks the suspicious quotes from a transcript and an evaluator scores only those quotes and the task; giving the scorer less context caught more sabotage (+16.8 points TPR at 1% FPR over the best baseline, self-reported) because full-context monitors talk themselves out of suspicion.
- [[Extreme-Risk Model Evaluation]] — Shevlane et al.'s 2023 framework splits evaluation for extreme risks into dangerous-capability and alignment (propensity) tests, names nine dangerous capabilities, and wires results into training, deployment, transparency and security decisions; a position paper with no experiments, it lists its own limits and hazards, including that training models to pass the tests removes their value as a risk indicator.
- [[FACTS Leaderboard]] — Google's four-part factuality suite (grounding to a document, closed-book parametric recall, search use, image-grounded answers) averaged into one FACTS Score; Gemini 3 Pro led at 68.8 over 15 proprietary models (self-reported), with the authors' judges mostly Google models.
- [[FActScore]] — A long-form factual-precision metric: split a generation into atomic facts and report the share a knowledge source supports; on 183 people biographies it showed ChatGPT falling from 80% to 16% as entities got rarer, and an automatic estimator came within 2% error of the human score (self-reported).
- [[FEVER Fact Verification Dataset]] — FEVER holds 185,445 Wikipedia-derived claims labelled supported, refuted or not enough info with sentence-level evidence, plus a retrieve-select-entail baseline.
- [[FINDER and DEFT (Deep Research Agent Failures)]] — A 100-task checklist benchmark (FINDER) and a 14-category failure taxonomy (DEFT) built by coding about 1,000 reports from 12 deep research agents; generation failures, led by fabricated content, were the largest class, ahead of retrieval and reasoning (self-reported, LLM-assisted labelling).
- [[FLASK Skill-Set Evaluation]] — FLASK scores each model answer on 12 separate skills with hand-written 1-5 rubrics instead of one overall grade; with a GPT-4 grader, skill-specific rubrics reached Spearman 0.680 with human labels against 0.641 for a skill-agnostic prompt on 200 instances (self-reported), and removing the reference answer hurt most.
- [[FollowIR Instruction-Following Retrieval Benchmark]] — FollowIR re-annotates three TREC collections with long instructions and measures with p-MRR whether retrievers change their ranking when an instruction changes; most embedders scored near or below zero (self-reported).
- [[Forecasting Rare Model Behaviours]] — Fit a Gumbel-tail line to the ten highest per-query elicitation scores of a 100-1,000 query eval to forecast the worst-case risk at up to roughly 1,000 times as many queries; the mean absolute log10 error on misuse worst-query risk was 1.7 against 2.4 for a log-normal fit (self-reported).
- [[FRAMES Benchmark]] — FRAMES is an 824-question multi-hop Wikipedia benchmark that scores factuality, retrieval and reasoning together; the paper's multi-step BM25 loop with search-planning prompts took Gemini-1.5-Pro from 0.408 to 0.66.
- [[FreshQA]] — A 600-question benchmark of never-, slow-, fast-changing and false-premise questions with RELAXED and STRICT grading; in April 2023 GPT-4 scored 28.6% STRICT with no search and 75.6% with FreshPrompt search-result injection (self-reported), with the STRICT-RELAXED gap read as the hallucination measure.
- [[Functional Benchmarks and the Reasoning Gap]] — Rewrite benchmark problems as code that regenerates them with new inputs each quarter, and count a problem as solved only if the static version and every snapshot are right; on 2,060 functionalised MATH problems the 13 models tested lost 58.35% to 80.31% of their static accuracy (self-reported).
- [[GAIA General-Assistant Benchmark]] — GAIA is a 2023 set of 466 human-written questions with unique short answers that are conceptually simple for people but need multi-step tool use; humans scored 92% against 15% for GPT-4 with plugins (self-reported), and every system scored 0 on Level 3.
- [[Gaia2]] — Meta's benchmark of 1,120 scenarios in an asynchronous simulated environment where the clock keeps running while the agent thinks, graded by a verifier that checks only write actions; GPT-5 (high) scored 42.1 pass@1 overall but 0.0 on the Time split (self-reported).
- [[GAMUT Meta-Rubric Benchmark]] — GAMUT is a 1,813-question multimodal benchmark of factual completeness whose rubrics are written as structured meta-rubrics (lists, processes, importance tiers) and compiled by fixed rules into flat binary checks for a judge; the best of 14 models scored 58.7% under one judge and no flat-versus-structured comparison was run (self-reported).
- [[GEMBA Translation Quality Prompting]] — GEMBA prompts a GPT model once per translation segment for a quality score and averages segments into a system score; with GPT-4 it topped the WMT22 metrics at system level on three language pairs but only trailed the best metrics at segment level (self-reported).
- [[Generative Search Engine Verifiability Audit]] — A 2023 human audit of four commercial answer engines scoring each sentence's citations for recall and precision; the engines were fluent but often unsupported, and citation precision moved opposite to perceived utility.
- [[GHOSTS Benchmark]] — A set of 709 graduate-level maths prompts (proofs, integrals, olympiad problems, search-style queries) graded 1-5 by expert mathematicians with error codes; ChatGPT averaged 3.20 and 3.29 on two January 2023 versions and GPT-4 4.15 on a 170-prompt subset (self-reported), under a pass line of 3.5.
- [[Goal-Directedness Measure]] — Everitt et al. score goal-directedness as how much of an LLM's measured capability it actually uses on a larger task, on a scale from a random policy to full use; in a Blocksworld test of 8 models, most fell short, and measurements taken fell when a step sat inside a bigger task (self-reported).
- [[GPQA]] — A set of graduate-level biology, physics and chemistry multiple-choice questions written by PhD-level contractors and kept only if experts agree on the answer while skilled non-experts with web access mostly fail; the non-experts reached 34% and the best baseline, GPT-4 few-shot chain of thought, 38.7% on the extended set (self-reported).
- [[GPTScore]] — GPTScore rates a generated text by the language-model probability of that text under a prompt stating the task and aspect, with no training; across 19 backbones, 4 tasks and 37 datasets the authors found instructions and demonstrations help on some aspects and not others, and text-davinci-003 beat -001 in summarisation but not in dialogue (self-reported).
- [[GSM-Symbolic]] — A template-based rebuild of 100 GSM8K questions that can be re-instantiated with new names and numbers; across 25 models the score on one question set was a draw from a distribution, adding clauses lowered it, and one irrelevant clause (GSM-NoOp) cost o1-preview 17.5 points (self-reported).
- [[Harness-IF]] — A benchmark of rule-level instruction following in multi-turn coding agents that scores against-prior rules (ones the model would not follow by default) separately; across 12 models accuracy ran 72.1% to 85.9% but against-prior accuracy was lower for every model, by 3.6 to 7.4 points (mean 5.81), and a small pilot found tool and skill descriptions lose instruction conflicts (self-reported).
- [[HELM Holistic Evaluation]] — HELM scores 30 language models on 42 scenarios under one standardized prompting setup across seven metric categories, so that accuracy, robustness, fairness and cost can be read side by side; the paper's own ablations show prompt format and adaptation method moving accuracy as much as the model does (self-reported).
- [[Hidden-DFA Environment Inference Testbed]] — A procedurally generated, size-controlled testbed in which a tool-calling agent must learn a hidden automaton by querying it; success drops sharply with automaton size, and the paper grades failures by whether classic passive learners could have recovered the automaton from the agent's own evidence (self-reported, three models).
- [[Hidden-State Hallucination Probes]] — Small classifiers on a frozen model's hidden activations to tell true from false output: SAPLMA (Azaria and Mitchell) trained one on a 6,084-sentence true-false set, and WACK (Simhi et al.) builds per-model datasets that separate hallucinations from missing knowledge (HK-) from hallucinations despite knowing (HK+), where probes reached 60-70% in the best settings (self-reported).
- [[Holistic Agent Leaderboard (HAL)]] — HAL is a shared harness that ran 21,730 agent rollouts (9 models, 9 benchmarks, about $40,000, self-reported), mostly single runs; it found higher reasoning effort gave equal or lower accuracy in 21 of 36 pairs, the costliest model on the cost-accuracy frontier in 1 of 9 benchmarks, and log analysis surfacing leakage, shortcuts and unsafe actions.
- [[HotpotQA]] — A crowdsourced multi-hop QA set where each question needs two Wikipedia paragraphs and carries sentence-level supporting facts; the 2018 baseline scores 58.28 answer F1 in the distractor setting but 34.36 in the full wiki setting, against 91.40 for humans (self-reported).
- [[IMO-Bench]] — A Google DeepMind suite of three Olympiad-level benchmarks (400 short-answer problems, 60 proof problems, 1,000 proof-grading items) with LLM autograders; the top model scored 80.0% on answers and 65.7% on advanced proofs while non-Gemini models stayed below 25% on advanced proofs (self-reported, with the lab's own models and judge).
- [[InterCode]] — A 2023 gym-style framework that wraps Bash, SQL and Python execution so an LLM can retry with execution feedback; on Spider SQL, GPT-4 rose from 9.1% single-turn to 73.7% with Try Again (self-reported), though the retry loop sees the reward computed against the gold answer.
- [[Intrinsic vs Extrinsic Hallucination]] — The NLG hallucination survey by Ji and colleagues defines intrinsic hallucination as output that contradicts the source and extrinsic as output the source can neither support nor contradict, then reviews metrics and mitigations; nearly every number is quoted from earlier work.
- [[J1 Reinforcement-Learned Judge]] — J1 trains a judge to write out its reasoning before a verdict using online reinforcement learning with a reward for matching the known-better response, plus a consistency reward for answer-order swaps; its 32B model scored 76.8 on PPE Correctness, 10.3 above its base model (self-reported), yet trained pairwise judges still flipped verdicts on 14.5-21.9% of swapped pairs.
- [[JEV-as-a-Judge Cascade]] — A decision-only cheap judge (JEV) answers when its confidence clears a threshold and escalates the rest to GPT-6; on 1,610 preference pairs the frozen cascade scored 93.4% against 92.5% for GPT-6 alone at 41.4% of the fee (self-reported), but the saving was large on RewardBench and small on JudgeBench, and confidence routing failed on reference-free prose.
- [[Judge Lifecycle with RART and a Drift Band]] — A production-judge lifecycle from a Netflix workshop paper: a rationale-annotated benchmark, RART rubric tuning that scores specificity 3:1:1, a bounded-retry revision gate, and a weekly human sample that triggers re-tuning when the judge falls more than 2 standard deviations below the rater mean; the re-tune path never fired online (self-reported).
- [[KILT Benchmark]] — KILT maps 11 knowledge-intensive datasets across 5 tasks onto one 2019 Wikipedia snapshot with provenance, and its joint scores pay out only when the right page is ranked first; RAG-style baselines beat closed-book BART on most tasks except ELI5 (self-reported).
- [[Knowledge Index and Information Gain]] — Two step-level scores for a chain of thought, Knowledge Index (are the facts right) and Information Gain (does the step move toward the answer), reported to separate what accuracy hides: on five medical benchmarks the Qwen R1-distilled model had similar Information Gain to its base but lower Knowledge Index and lower accuracy (self-reported).
- [[LAMA Knowledge Probe]] — LAMA turns facts into cloze statements to test what relational knowledge pretrained language models hold with no fine-tuning; BERT-large rivals an oracle relation-extraction KB on some relations and trails on N-to-M ones (self-reported).
- [[Language Model Self-Knowledge]] — Two 2022-23 ways to test whether a model knows what it knows: Kadavath et al. have models score their own samples (P(True)) or train a head to predict whether they will answer correctly (P(IK)), and Yin et al.'s SelfAware set (1,032 unanswerable questions) scores whether a model voices uncertainty, with GPT-4 below a human F1 of 84.93 (self-reported).
- [[LegalBench]] — A benchmark of 162 legal-reasoning tasks from 36 sources, built by a call for tasks and grouped into six reasoning types; GPT-4 led the category averages of 20 models (self-reported), rule recall was the weakest category for every model, and the choice of in-context demonstrations moved scores by over 20 points.
- [[LFQA Attribution and SALAD]] — SALAD is a sentence-level human attribution dataset for long-form QA answers; with evidence documents in context, many sentences are supported and the last sentence is the likeliest to be unsupported, and a T5 NLI judge is the best automatic detector (self-reported).
- [[LILA Math Reasoning Benchmark]] — LILA unifies 20 math datasets into 23 tasks with Python program solutions, plus out-of-distribution and robustness splits; multi-task fine-tuning of GPT-Neo-2.7B beat single-task training and few-shot Codex scored only 60.40% F1 (self-reported).
- [[Live Multi-Market Trading Benchmark]] — Qian et al.'s live benchmark runs four trading-agent designs on five LLM backbones across two stocks and two cryptocurrencies from August to September 2025, one BUY/SELL/HOLD decision a day; the best cell (40.83% on TSLA) trails buy-and-hold (46.88%), each cell is one run, and the authors' architecture-over-backbone claim rests on identical rows (self-reported).
- [[Live Penetration Testing of Agents and Professionals]] — Lin et al. ran AI agents and 10 professionals on a live university network (about 8,000 hosts); the authors' own ARTEMIS scaffold ranked second of 15 entries, scoring 95.2 against 111.4 for the best human, with one run each and no statistical testing (self-reported).
- [[LiveMCP-101]] — A benchmark of 101 multi-step tasks over live MCP tools (41 servers, 260 tools) that grades an agent against a reference agent run at the same time; GPT-5 had the highest task success rate at 58.42% across 18 LLMs (self-reported), and semantic parameter errors were the largest failure type.
- [[LLM Bias and Fairness Survey]] — Gallegos et al.'s survey (Computational Linguistics 50:3) organises LLM bias evaluation into embedding, probability and generated-text metrics and mitigation into pre-, in-, intra- and post-processing, and lists the weaknesses of each.
- [[LLM Hallucination Surveys]] — Two 2023-24 surveys carve LLM hallucination up differently (factuality versus faithfulness in one; input-, context- and fact-conflicting in the other); neither runs experiments, and both compile benchmark sizes, detection families and mitigations from cited work.
- [[LLM Trustworthiness Taxonomy]] — A 2023 survey sorts LLM alignment into seven trustworthiness dimensions with 29 sub-categories and measures eight sub-categories on six LLMs with LLM-generated tests and a gpt-4 judge, concluding that more-aligned models do better overall but unevenly by category (self-reported).
- [[LLM-as-a-Verifier]] — Instead of reading the single score token a judge emits, take the probability-weighted expectation over its score-token logprobs, which removes ties between candidates; on Terminal-Bench V2 trajectory pairs one such verifier pass scored 74.7% pairwise accuracy against 71.8% for a discrete judge, and best-of-N selection with it recovered part of the gap to an oracle (self-reported).
- [[LM Red Teaming]] — Red teaming with a language model has a red LM write test cases, a target LM answer them and a classifier flag harmful replies; in Perez et al.'s 2022 study on a 280B chatbot, zero-shot generation found 18,444 offensive replies and RL raised the hit rate above 40% at a cost in diversity (self-reported).
- [[LMSYS-Chat-1M]] — One million real user conversations with 25 LLMs collected from the Vicuna demo and Chatbot Arena in 2023, with four demonstrated uses: a fine-tuned Vicuna-7B moderator matching GPT-4 on a 110-message set, a 50-conversation jailbreak benchmark, instruction tuning, and the Arena-Hard-200 prompt set (self-reported).
- [[Long-Form QA Evaluation Hurdles]] — Krishna et al.'s 2021 control experiments on long-form QA (ELI5, KILT): random retrievals barely move ROUGE-L, a copied question beats a retrieval-augmented system on it, and humans prefer gold answers 68% to 14%, so leaderboard gains there say little about answer quality (self-reported).
- [[LongBench]] — LongBench is a bilingual multitask long-context benchmark of 21 datasets in 6 categories, with a length-balanced LongBench-E subset; it also found retrieval helped a 4k model but hurt long-context models (self-reported).
- [[Lookback Lens]] — A contextual-hallucination detector that feeds one number per attention head, the share of attention going to the supplied context, into a logistic regression; on LLaMA-2-7B-Chat it reached AUROC 85.3 transferring QA to summarisation (self-reported), but only about 66 in the realistic chunk-by-chunk setting, and it needs white-box access.
- [[MACHIAVELLI Benchmark]] — 134 text games with 572,322 scenes labelled by GPT-4 for utility, power and 13 ethical violation types, used to chart reward against harm; a reward-trained RL agent scored about double a random agent's reward with 108% of its violations, and steering methods gave only modest gains (self-reported).
- [[MAG-V Trajectory Verification]] — MAG-V generates synthetic test questions with an investigator agent, then verifies a tool-calling agent's trajectory by asking it three reverse-engineered alternate questions and feeding trajectory similarity features to a classical classifier; on 45 annotated trajectories, k-NN matched a GPT-4 judge (82.33 vs 82.83 accuracy) (self-reported).
- [[Math Word Problem Benchmarks]] — Four 2017 to 2021 math word problem datasets (AQuA-RAT, MathQA, ASDiv, SVAMP) and what each paper found: noisy rationales, annotation errors, low lexical diversity, and solvers that answer without reading the question; all with pre-LLM solvers, so none of it is a statement about prompted LLMs.
- [[MAUVE]] — MAUVE scores the gap between model text and human text by quantizing embeddings of both and taking the area under a divergence curve; the paper reports it ranks decoders and model sizes as expected where generation perplexity does not (self-reported).
- [[MedBrowseComp]] — A benchmark of 1,000+ multi-hop medical fact-seeking questions built from HemOnc.org and linked sources; accuracy falls as hop count rises, search helps most at low hop counts, and computer-use agents trail API-driven deep research (self-reported, one run per system).
- [[MEDEC]] — A benchmark of clinical notes that are either correct or hold exactly one medical error of five types, with three subtasks (flag, locate, correct); LLMs lead on detection or correction in different ways and two doctors still beat all models.
- [[Min-K% Prob Pretraining-Data Detection]] — Min-K% Prob scores a text by the average log-probability of its lowest-probability tokens to guess whether a model trained on it; on the WikiMIA benchmark it averaged AUC 0.72 against 0.67 for perplexity, and caught about a fifth of members at a 5% false-positive rate (self-reported), which bounds how far it can certify an eval set as clean.
- [[MINT Multi-Turn Benchmark]] — A 2023 benchmark of 586 instances where an LLM solves tasks over up to five turns by running Python and receiving GPT-4-simulated language feedback; across 20 LLMs, feedback added 2-17% absolute and SIFT or RLHF often lowered multi-turn scores (self-reported).
- [[MLE-bench]] — OpenAI's benchmark of 75 Kaggle competitions that an agent must solve end to end, scored by whether its submission would have won a medal on the private leaderboard; the best agent medalled in 16.9% of competitions at one attempt, and the scaffold mattered far more than hardware or extra time.
- [[Model Version Drift]] — A hosted model behind the same name can change between snapshots: Chen et al. found GPT-4's prime-number accuracy fall from 84% to 51% between March and June 2023 and its directly runnable code from 52% to 10%, mostly through format and instruction-following changes, while 12% of medical-exam answers changed although accuracy barely moved.
- [[Model-Written Evaluations]] — A language model writes labelled evaluation examples from a behaviour description and a preference model filters them; crowdworkers rated 93-95.5% of the labels correct across datasets, and the 154 resulting datasets surfaced sycophancy and RLHF-driven inverse scaling (self-reported).
- [[Multi-News]] — A multi-document news summarization dataset of 56,216 article-summary pairs built from editor-written summaries and their cited sources, released with Hi-MAP, a pointer-generator that rescales attention with a learned MMR score.
- [[MultiMedQA and Med-PaLM]] — MultiMedQA combines six medical QA datasets with a new 3,173-question consumer set and a clinician and lay rating framework; Flan-PaLM 540B reached 67.6% on MedQA and instruction prompt tuning (Med-PaLM) cut clinician-rated potentially harmful answers from 29.7% to 5.9% on 140 questions (self-reported).
- [[Muses-Bench Multi-User Agent Benchmark]] — A benchmark of 19 models acting as one shared agent for several users with different authority, covering conflicting instructions, prompt-level access control over turns, and meeting scheduling.
- [[NatureBench]] — A benchmark of 90 containerised tasks built from Nature-family papers with the source method withheld, scored by the gap to the published result; the top agent matched it on 47.8% of tasks, and successes were mostly recast as generic prediction or engineering problems (self-reported, one run per agent).
- [[NeedleBench]] — NeedleBench is a bilingual synthetic long-context benchmark with information-sparse needle tasks and an information-dense Ancestral Trace Challenge; retrieval is near-solved at 128K while multi-needle reasoning is not (self-reported).
- [[OlympiadBench]] — OlympiadBench is 8,476 bilingual, multimodal Olympiad-level math and physics problems with expert solutions; zero-shot, the best model (GPT-4V) scored 17.97% on the auto-scorable open-ended subset in the authors' own run (self-reported).
- [[Output Length Confounds Reference Metrics]] — N-gram and LLM-judge scores on open-ended tasks move with how long the answer is, not only how good it is; L-Eval found that telling models the expected answer length lifted ROUGE-L's agreement with human rankings from Kendall tau 0.5 to 0.8, so control length before comparing systems.
- [[PandaLM]] — PandaLM fine-tunes a LLaMA-based judge on 300K order-consistent pairwise labels distilled from GPT-3.5 and uses it to pick instruction-tuning hyperparameters; the 70B size agreed with humans slightly more than GPT-4 on a 999-pair test set while the 7B size scored 0.5926 and stayed below GPT-3.5 and GPT-4 (self-reported).
- [[PaperBench]] — OpenAI's benchmark in which an agent replicates 20 ICML 2024 papers from scratch and is graded by an LLM judge walking a weighted rubric tree of 8,316 binary leaves; o1 with an iterative agent scored 24.4% against 41.4% for ML PhDs on a 3-paper subset (self-reported).
- [[Perplexity and Bits-per-Byte]] — Language-modeling metrics measure how well a model predicts text: entropy and cross entropy in bits, perplexity as their exponential, bits-per-byte as the tokenizer-independent version; useful for contamination checks and deduplication, a poor proxy once a model is post-trained or quantized.
- [[PERSONA-CHAT]] — A crowd-sourced dialogue dataset in which each speaker plays a written persona, used to test whether profile-conditioned chit-chat models predict the next utterance better and seem more engaging; persona conditioning helped ranking models but not the generative one in the human study.
- [[PIE Performance-Improving Edits Benchmark]] — PIE is a dataset of more than 77K pairs of slow and fast C++ programs timed in the gem5 simulator; the paper reports that fine-tuned and retrieval-prompted models reach multi-fold speedups, that chain-of-thought prompting adds little, and that conditioning on speed lowers correctness.
- [[PlanBench o1 Evaluation]] — A September 2024 research note that runs OpenAI's o1-preview and o1-mini on the PlanBench Blocksworld and Mystery Blocksworld sets, with accuracy, cost, time and unsolvable-instance results; a classical planner scores 600/600 at a small fraction of the cost.
- [[Position Bias Calibration (MEC, BPC, HITLC)]] — Three calibrations for pairwise LLM judges whose verdicts flip with answer order: sample evidence-first judgments (MEC), average over both orders (BPC), and send high-entropy cases to humans (HITLC); on 80 Vicuna questions they lifted GPT-4 agreement with a three-author majority from 52.7% to 62.5% without humans (self-reported).
- [[Price Reversal in Reasoning-Model Costs]] — Chen et al. measured the total cost of 8 reasoning models on 12 tasks and found that in 106 of 336 pairwise comparisons (31.5%, self-reported) the model with the lower listed per-token price cost more; the authors attribute over 95% of the gap on reversed single-turn pairs to thinking tokens, and show per-query cost for one model and query varies up to 9.7 times.
- [[Private Variant Testing on Arena Leaderboards]] — Singh et al. argue that Chatbot Arena's open-ended policy lets a provider test many private variants and publish only the best: two identical checkpoints scored 1052 and 1069, a simulation puts the gain from picking the best of 20 variants at about 50 points, and they estimate Google and OpenAI received about 40% of all battles (all self-reported, with the authors' disclosed conflict of interest).
- [[Prometheus 2 Evaluator Models]] — Prometheus 2 is an open evaluator model made by training one judge for direct 1-5 rubric scoring and another for pairwise ranking, then merging their weights; on FLASK its 8x7B version reached 0.555 Pearson with humans against 0.449 for the first Prometheus and 0.679 for GPT-4 (self-reported), and merging beat joint training.
- [[PromptBench]] — A modular Python library that unifies LLM evaluation across standard, dynamic (DyVal) and semantic (MSTemp) protocols, adversarial prompt attacks at four levels and six prompt-engineering methods, with 12 tasks and 22 datasets; its leaderboards are given only as figures (self-reported).
- [[PrOntoQA]] — A synthetic first-order-logic question set over fictional ontologies, with unique parseable proofs, used to check chain-of-thought step by step rather than by final label.
- [[PubMedQA]] — A biomedical yes/no/maybe QA set built from PubMed question-titled abstracts, with 1k expert labels; the best 2019 BioBERT setup reaches 68.1% accuracy against 78.0% for one human in the reasoning-required setting (self-reported).
- [[Ragas Reference-Free RAG Metrics]] — Ragas scores a RAG pipeline on faithfulness, answer relevance and context relevance by prompting gpt-3.5-turbo-16k, with no reference answers; on the 50-question WikiEval set it agreed with human pairwise preference 0.95, 0.78 and 0.70 of the time, above two direct-prompting baselines (self-reported).
- [[RAGChecker]] — A claim-level RAG diagnosis framework that extracts claims from the response and the reference answer and checks entailment, reporting overall, retriever and generator metrics; its overall metrics correlated with human preference at Pearson 61.93 on 280 response pairs against 70.09 between annotators (self-reported).
- [[RAGEval and DragonBall]] — RAGEval generates scenario-specific RAG test sets from a schema and scores answers with Completeness, Hallucination, Irrelevance, Recall and EIR; DragonBall is the resulting 6,711-question finance, law and medical set in Chinese and English (self-reported).
- [[ReClor Logical Reasoning Reading Comprehension]] — ReClor is 6,138 GMAT and LSAT logical reasoning questions with an EASY split (solvable from the options alone) and a HARD split; fine-tuned models scored about 75% on EASY and about 30% on HARD in the authors' runs, showing biased options can inflate scores (self-reported).
- [[Repository-Level Coding Benchmarks]] — Three 2025-26 benchmarks that stretch SWE-bench beyond single-issue Python fixes: SWE-EVO (48 release-to-release tasks, best 25% resolved), SWE-PolyBench (2,110 tasks in four languages, best 14.1% overall) and LongCLI-Bench (20 long command-line tasks, best 16.7%); all self-reported.
- [[REST Multi-Problem Stress Testing]] — Concatenate s consecutive benchmark questions into one prompt and score each; on MATH500 a 1.6-point gap between two distilled 7B and 32B reasoning models became 22.22 points under stress, and DeepSeek-R1's AIME24 accuracy fell from 81.66 to 52.49 (self-reported).
- [[ROSCOE Reasoning Chain Metrics]] — ROSCOE scores a model's step-by-step reasoning chain without a gold chain, using 18 metrics built on step-to-context embedding alignment, NLI contradiction and fluency; it beat ROUGE-style and other baselines on synthetic perturbation tests, with moderate correlations on small human-judged sets (self-reported).
- [[SAFE Long-Form Factuality Scoring]] — SAFE splits a long answer into atomic facts, searches Google for each and labels it supported, not supported or irrelevant; F1@K then combines the counts. It agreed with crowd annotators on 72.0% of 16,011 biography facts and cost $0.19 per response against $4 for humans (self-reported), with the LongFact prompt set used to rank 13 models.
- [[SciBench]] — SciBench has 869 college textbook science and math problems (177 multimodal) plus a closed set of exam problems, scored by numeric answers, with an LLM verifier assigning each error to one of ten missing skills; GPT-4 with few-shot Python tools was best, and no prompting strategy won on all skills (self-reported).
- [[ScienceAgentBench]] — A benchmark of 102 data-driven discovery tasks taken from 44 peer-reviewed papers, where an agent must write one self-contained Python program; the best agents solve about a third of tasks best-of-3 and far fewer on average, and most failures are programs that run but compute the wrong thing.
- [[ScienceWorld Benchmark]] — ScienceWorld is an interactive text environment of 30 elementary-science tasks over a simulator with thermodynamics, circuits, chemistry and biology; five trained agents averaged between 0.05 and 0.17 on unseen variations, and a 1.5M-parameter interactive agent beat 11B models trained on static data (self-reported).
- [[Self-Critiquing Models for Human Evaluators]] — Saunders et al. fine-tune language models to write critiques of summaries and find that showing eight model critiques lets human labelers find about 50 percent more flaws, including 45 percent against 27 percent of deliberately planted ones (self-reported, 2022-era models).
- [[Self-Supervised LLM Evaluation by Text Transformations]] — Score a model's sensitivity or invariance to a simple transformation of any text corpus (negation, a toxic trigger, shuffling, splitting tokens) and compare against labelled benchmarks; on Wikipedia sentences the scores tracked TriviaQA and LAMBADA closely in scatter plots, with no correlation coefficients (self-reported).
- [[Self-Taught Evaluators]] — Self-Taught Evaluators trains a pairwise judge with no human preference labels: it builds a worse response by answering a modified instruction, keeps judgments that pick the right side, and repeats; Llama3-70B-Instruct went from 75.4 to 88.3 on RewardBench (self-reported), with a 5.6-point swing from answer order alone.
- [[SelfCheckGPT]] — A black-box hallucination detector that samples the model several times and scores each sentence of the main answer by how far the samples disagree with it; on 238 GPT-3 WikiBio passages the LLM-prompt variant was best on the AUC-PR and passage-correlation measures reported (self-reported), but it detects inconsistency, not falsehood.
- [[Shepherd Critic Model]] — Shepherd is a LLaMA-7B fine-tuned on about 8K curated community critiques to point out errors in another model's answer; GPT-4 pairwise judging favoured it 87.0 against Alpaca-7B but near-tied it with SelFee-7B and ChatGPT (self-reported), and the same paper found GPT-4 Likert scoring disagreed with humans.
- [[Silicon Sampling and Algorithmic Fidelity]] — Conditioning a language model on real survey respondents' first-person backstories to get one synthetic respondent per person; Argyle et al. report GPT-3 matched human subgroup patterns in US politics, with strong partisans well predicted and pure independents not (group-level only).
- [[Skill Lift Evaluation]] — Measure an agent skill by running the same tasks with and without it and reporting the difference (skill lift); ACES (NVIDIA authors) averaged +0.2134 composite lift over 947 paired cases while static scan scores had no measurable correlation with live lift, and SkillsBench reported curated skills lifting mean pass rate from 33.9% to 50.5% over 18 model-harness configurations (all self-reported).
- [[Spider Text-to-SQL Benchmark]] — Spider is a human-labeled text-to-SQL dataset of 10,181 questions over 200 multi-table databases, split so test databases are unseen; in 2018 the best baseline reached 9.7% exact match on that split against 34.3% when databases were shared (self-reported).
- [[SPoC Pseudocode-to-Code]] — SPoC pairs 18,356 C++ programs with line-level human pseudocode and about 38.6 tests each, and searches over per-line translations under a compilation budget; the paper reports error localization lifting success to 44.7% at 100 compilations, though its abstract and introduction disagree on the top-one baseline (self-reported).
- [[SQuAD]] — A span-extraction reading-comprehension benchmark of 107,785 crowdsourced questions on 536 Wikipedia articles, scored by exact match and token F1 against humans; the CNN/Daily Mail cloze corpus is its large semi-synthetic predecessor.
- [[Summarization and Translation Hallucination Studies]] — Two pre-LLM studies of hallucination in generated text: a human annotation of five XSum systems found hallucinations in over 70% of summaries and entailment as the best automatic proxy, and a token-level detector trained on synthetic noise reached average F1 near 0.6 on translation and summarization sets (self-reported).
- [[Summary Factual Consistency Metrics]] — Three ways to check whether a summary's claims are supported by its source: FactCC (a BERT classifier trained on rule-made corruptions), QAGS (ask questions of the summary, answer them from summary and source, compare) and TrueTeacher (an LLM labels real model summaries to train a smaller checker); each paper reports its own gains (self-reported).
- [[SummHay Benchmark]] — SummHay asks a model to summarise the insights shared across a synthetic 100-document haystack (about 93K tokens) and cite the source documents, scoring coverage, citation and their product; no tested system came near the human estimate of 56.1 (self-reported).
- [[SUSVIBES (Vibe-Coding Security Benchmark)]] — A benchmark of 186 repository-level feature requests, each originally implemented with a vulnerability, that scores agent code on functional and security tests together; the best settings pass functional tests on over half the tasks but pass security too on about one in eight (self-reported, pass@1).
- [[SWE-Lancer]] — OpenAI's benchmark of 1,488 real Upwork software tasks worth $1,000,000 in payouts, graded by hidden end-to-end browser tests and scored in dollars earned; Claude 3.5 Sonnet earned $208k of $501k on the public Diamond split (self-reported).
- [[Taste-Bench]] — A 502-question benchmark of decision forks mined from agent trajectories, where a model must pick the better of two directions before the outcome is visible; the top of 14 models scored 59.7% against 25% for random under a both-orders rule, accuracy fell from 62.3% to 21.0% as the deciding evidence moved later, and a distilled student's advice raised a small executor's SWE-bench Pro success on 41 tasks (self-reported).
- [[Terminal-Bench]] — A benchmark of 89 manually verified command-line tasks (version 2.0) run in containers; across six agents and 16 models the best pair, Codex CLI with GPT-5.2, resolved under two-thirds of tasks (self-reported), and the same model scored differently under different scaffolds.
- [[Text Metrics Beyond N-gram Overlap]] — Four 2019-2020 metrics that replace word overlap with something else: BERTScore (greedy embedding matching), BLEURT (BERT pretrained on synthetic perturbations, then tuned on human ratings), CodeBLEU (BLEU plus syntax-tree and data-flow matches) and USR (reference-free dialog scoring); each reports gains on its own correlation setup (self-reported).
- [[Text-Game Agent Environments (Jericho, ALFWorld, CALM)]] — Three 2020-era pieces of the text-game line of agent evaluation: Jericho (an interface to human-made interactive fiction, with template actions), CALM (a language model trained on human play that proposes candidate actions) and ALFWorld (text games mirroring embodied household tasks, where text training transferred to the embodied side); all numbers self-reported, RL-era agents only.
- [[The Augmentation Gate]] — The A in RAG is the gate nobody evaluates — retrieval can be perfect and generation sound while the failure lives entirely in how much retrieved context got handed to the model.
- [[TheAgentCompany]] — A benchmark of 175 workplace tasks in a self-hosted simulated software company, scored by checkpoints with partial credit; the best agent fully completed 30.3% (OpenHands with Gemini 2.5 Pro, self-reported), at about 27 steps and $4 per task.
- [[TheoremQA]] — An 800-question university-level benchmark built around 354 theorems in math, physics, EE&CS and finance with short exact-match answers; GPT-4 with Program-of-Thoughts led at 52.4% (self-reported) and open instruction-tuned models stayed under 15%.
- [[TIR-Judge]] — TIR-Judge trains a judge that writes and runs Python while judging, using end-to-end reinforcement learning; on RewardBench2 listwise best-of-4 its 8B Zero variant averaged 73.4 against 76.5 for Claude-Opus-4 (self-reported), while merely prompting Qwen3 with the same code tool gave negligible or negative gains.
- [[Tool-Augmented Factuality Checking]] — Check a generated answer by splitting it into short claims, generating a search or code query per claim, running the tool and asking a model whether the evidence agrees; FacTool beat a model checking itself in every domain it tested (science citations 94.7 vs 21.5 F1 with GPT-4, self-reported), because self-checks are too lenient.
- [[Trajectory Over Outcome]] — Why an agent must be scored on the path and not just the answer — a correct output from a lucky trajectory is a latent failure, and routing and stopping point are invisible to an outcome score.

- [[Agentic KPI Trees]] — KPI tree pattern for agentic products — goal completion rate, no-touch rate, and transaction match accuracy as the primary success metrics for product-assistant, document processing, and reconciliation agents.
- [[Anthropic Three-Tier Eval Taxonomy]] — Practical agent evaluation framework from Anthropic — three tiers (unit/trajectory/e2e) mapped to cost, determinism, and failure coverage. Unit covers ~70% of regressions cheaply; trajectory checks routing paths; e2e is sparingly used for quality gates.
- [[Conversational Test Fixture Design]] — How to author a fixture for a pipeline whose input is dialogue — the volunteer's verbatim words as the input unit, the full expected artifact as the oracle, and a short "key validation points" list naming the specific wrong answers the scenario exists to catch.
- [[Copilot Learning Loop]] — The operational process for improving agent systems over time — signal capture from real usage, knowledge refinement workflows, and controlled autonomy expansion. Not automatic: requires deliberate instrumentation and tooling.
- [[Direct Preference Optimization]] — Training-time technique that fine-tunes a model on human preference pairs (preferred vs rejected responses) without a reward model — replaces PPO/RLHF for preference alignment. Not applicable to API-only models.
- [[Eval-Driven Development (EDD)]] — Writing the eval suite before the agent exists — ATDD reconstructed for non-deterministic systems, where first-ness buys honesty rather than design pressure.
- [[RAG Eval Gate Contract]] — Eight-gate ownership contract for RAG evaluation pipelines — each gate answers a distinct question about corpus quality, retrieval, generation, and grader calibration, with strict handoff contracts between gates.
- [[Eval Harness Anatomy]] — The vocabulary of agent evaluation — task, trial, grader, trajectory, outcome — plus the separation that makes it work: the evaluation harness treats the agent harness as the system under test, and capability evals want low pass rates while regression evals want ~100%.
- [[Eval Maturity Ladder]] — Five levels of what eval infrastructure exists — vibes, deterministic gates, separated evaluator, eval sets plus tracing, continuous sampling with drift alerts — where most builders sit at 0 and production demands 3+, with trajectory-over-outcome and cost-per-success as the metrics that expose thrashing.
- [[Eval Non-Determinism]] — Why one trial is an anecdote — pass@k when a single success suffices, pass^k when consistency is the product, and the arithmetic that makes a "75% reliable" agent pass three consecutive trials only 42% of the time.
- [[Eval Suite Maintenance]] — Fix the evaluation system before changing the agent, read traces rather than scores, and treat a saturated suite as a stopped learning signal — paired regression and rolling-discovery sets keep the frontier moving.
- [[Eval Ladder]] — A four-rung progression — manual review, golden-set grading, LLM-judge, user feedback — sequenced so each rung's failures supply the next rung's test cases, with an explicit "most POCs reach rung 2–3 and that's sufficient" stopping point.
- [[Eval vs Test Distinction]] — A test tells you your code is broken; an eval tells you your product got worse — two different instruments with different targets, graders, cadences, and failure semantics.
- [[Experiment Tracking Schemas]] — The metadata contract that makes an eval run reproducible and diffable — base trace fields, ExperimentRun/RagConfig/BedrockConfig/ChunkRecord, the instrumentation asymmetry between a custom pipeline and a managed KB, and the log-only-then-promote policy for grounding checks.
- [[Forecast Grader Thresholds]] — The pass/fail contract for time-series forecast evaluation — MASE against a naïve baseline, SMAPE, directional accuracy, and prediction-interval coverage — with the diagnostic each failure points to and the drift ratio that triggers retraining.
- [[Golden Set Mechanics]] — The shape of a golden case (input/expected/metadata), sizing by purpose (20–50 at spec time, 100–1000 for CI), sourcing priority, and the anti-staleness practices that keep a set measuring.
- [[Grounding Claim Methodology]] — Claims-based grounding — the "yellow highlighter" approach to RAG verification, where the agent extracts verbatim supporting quotes from retrieved documents before writing the final answer, creating a verifiable paper trail.
- [[Heuristic Pipeline Metrics]] — Automated measurement of operational health — latency, error rate, token usage, retrieval recall — that runs alongside every eval rung and is explicitly never sufficient alone, since a fast cheap wrong answer still fails.
- [[HITL Annotation Pipeline]] — Human-in-the-loop annotation workflow for conversation data — two-queue structure (random + edge case), inter-annotator agreement as quality gate, and feedback routing to eval dataset vs failure taxonomy.
- [[Human-Participant Skill Test Protocol]] — Usability-testing a conversational skill with real first-time users — pre-registered facilitator expectations, scripted plus real scenarios, non-intervention rules, and a severity-sorted friction log that feeds fixes back before the next session.
- [[LightGBM vs CatBoost Comparison]] — Methodology for comparing calibrated GBM rerankers head-to-head — fixing train/inference feature-distribution mismatch before comparing, native categorical handling, and Brier score/log-loss as the metrics that matter for calibrated probability scores.
- [[LLM-as-Judge Evaluation]] — A separate LLM scoring outputs against a rubric — the approach for subjective quality where exact matching fails, requiring calibration against human grades and recommended as a complement to golden-set grading rather than a replacement.
- [[LLM Grader Calibration Insights]] — Calibration evidence for LLM-as-judge graders in the project-g eval pipeline — custom v3 grader outperforms DeepEval defaults (+0.214 score delta vs +0.086), domain-shift is the main failure pattern, passage context is required for grounding accuracy. Grounding cross-check vs DeepEval shows near-zero agreement until article text is wired in.
- [[Manual Review as Eval Bootstrap]] — Human eyeballs on 10–20 real queries as the deliberate first eval rung — zero setup, doesn't scale, and valuable precisely because its failure patterns become the criteria every automated approach above it needs.
- [[Observability & Evaluation Glossary]] — Canonical vocabulary for agent observability and evaluation — the observability/tracking/tracing/monitoring/alerting hierarchy, offline vs online evaluation modes, heuristic vs LLM-judge metric types, dataset terminology, and rank-based retrieval metrics (MRR/precision@k/recall@k/ndcg@k/hit@k).
- [[Online Eval Sampling]] — Score 10–20% of production traces by rule rather than at random — negative feedback, high-cost dialogues (cost as a thrashing proxy), fixed time windows as the control group, and a full 48-hour review after any model or prompt change — with human labels calibrating an LLM judge that would otherwise drift.
- [[project-g Eval Architecture]] — Routing vs domain eval distinction (Strand A/E/F), grader interface contract, three-tier eval coverage, calibration methodology for the project-g HC agent eval pipeline, and ADK vs LangGraph parallel evaluation approach.
- [[RAG Eval Metrics Suite]] — Eight-metric RAG evaluation framework covering stakeholder quality (faithfulness, naturalness, completeness, relevance), retrieval quality (contextual relevance, recall, document precision), and system calibration — split between runtime-compatible and offline-only metrics.
- [[Skill Eval Pipeline (Blind Comparison + Grading)]] — Three-agent pipeline for A/B testing Claude Code skills — a blind comparator scores two outputs on a rubric without knowing which skill produced them, a grader checks explicit expectations pass/fail with cited evidence, and a post-hoc analyzer unblinds the result to explain why the winner won and suggest concrete improvements to the loser.
- [[Skill Pipeline Dryrun Testing]] — Regression-testing a chain of conversational skills by simulating a user through fixed scenarios with unambiguous expected outcomes — asserting not only what the pipeline produces but which questions it correctly skips and which it must still ask.
- [[Synthetic Dataset Generation for RAG Eval]] — Four-mode pipeline for generating and maintaining a versioned synthetic test dataset from a knowledge base — article fingerprinting drives incremental refresh, stable content-derived IDs make Langfuse upserts idempotent, and four query categories cover the full quality surface.
- [[System Design — Unified Eval Harness]] — Interview-format system design writeup of playground's eval harness — golden set → heuristic graders → LLM judges → gate, shared across three agent implementations, with HTML reporting and threshold governance.
- [[TruthfulQA]] — An 817-question benchmark written to elicit imitative falsehoods; in 2021-era models the best, GPT-3-175B with a helpful prompt, was truthful on 58% of questions against 94% for a human baseline (self-reported), and larger models within a family were generally less truthful.
- [[TyDi QA]] — A multilingual question-answering benchmark of 204K examples in 11 typologically diverse languages, with questions written natively from a short Wikipedia snippet and no translation, built so that question and answer overlap little.
- [[Universal Verifier for Computer-Use Agents]] — A prompted trajectory verifier for web computer-use agents built on four choices (task-only rubrics scored separately, process and outcome scored apart, controllable versus uncontrollable failures, per-criterion screenshot selection); with GPT-5.2 it reached outcome kappa 0.64 and 0.58 against human labels on two sets of 140 and 106 Fara-7B trajectories, above two earlier verifiers (self-reported).
- [[UQ Unsolved Questions Benchmark]] — A benchmark of 500 Stack Exchange questions that had no answers after at least two years, with no gold labels: LLM validators screen candidate answers and humans check the survivors. On the validator pass rate o3-pro cleared 15.0% and only 10 of the 91 human-checked passes were correct (self-reported).
- [[User Feedback Loops]] — Explicit ratings or implicit usage signals from deployed users — the only eval source that catches "technically correct but unhelpful", slow and sparse and biased, whose real payoff is converting thumbs-down cases into permanent golden-set entries.
- [[Eval Harness]] — Agent evaluation harness for assistant agents — four eval suites (routing, quality, behavioral, error handling), JSON evalset schema, tool_trajectory_avg_score metric, LLM-as-judge, Makefile flow, and CI regression gate. Production golden dataset: ~100 questions from 700-question Intercom set, Langfuse pipeline live, CS agent validated.
- [[UNGATED Metric Convention]] — record a metric as UNGATED (computed, reported, no threshold) until it has been observed enough to earn a gate
- [[Human Agreement Ceiling Protocol]] — Measure κ_HH before trusting an LLM judge on subjective dimensions (κ_judge ≥ 0.8 × κ_HH): weighted κ for ordinal, Krippendorff's α with MASI for multi-select, bootstrap CIs, n≈100, scene-literate raters recruited by name.
- [[Validated Concept vs Unmeasured System]] — The quoted 77.8% was a human-curation ceiling with an answer key, specs were read as shipped code, and launch gates were asserted everywhere and measured nowhere — label every number with what produced it.
- [[One Check Shape for Quality Verdicts]] — Nine unjoined quality flag vocabularies replaced by one Check shape writing to one `quality_verdicts` store keyed by `trace_id`; evals never score production rows, UNGATED is per component, trace is for debugging and the store is for rates.
- [[Append-Only Human Labels]] — persist rater judgments append-only, keyed to the enrichment row, with instrument column and fixed rater picklist so agreement stays measurable
- [[Frozen Version Stamps on Logged Results]] — stamp every served row with a version constant per component; logged output is not a logged rule; instrumentation ships with the feature
- [[Frozen Ranking Baseline from a Pure Ranker]] — a pure ranker makes an offline ranking baseline a composition of existing pieces; the real gaps are excluded-candidate export with reasons, slice breakdowns, and a version stamp per rule table
- [[Traffic-Gated Evaluation Ladder]] — For a pre-launch recommender, each evaluation method unlocks at a minimum data volume and can decide only certain questions — human blind rating now, LLM judge, offline replay (ranking only, never retrieval), interleaving, then A/B — so you climb the ladder instead of waiting for the top rung.
- [[Synthesize the Cheap Half]] — Synthetic data is safe for queries and (calibrated) judge labels, wrong for events, and forbidden for interactions — synthesize the side of the evaluation that is cheap to verify against reality, never the side that becomes the target.
- [[Decompose Low-Ceiling Dimensions into Checkable Sub-Signals]] — When humans cannot agree on a quality dimension (low κ ceiling), stop trying to rate it — decompose it into relational sub-signals that are facts in the data, turning a rating problem into a data-correctness problem, and cap the dimension's ranking weight by its ceiling meanwhile.
- [[Q0–Q5 Quality Ladder]] — A six-level per-feature quality ladder (unit/schema → golden set → LLM judge → adversarial/safety → human acceptance → production monitoring) where every level is owned by name, judges are never the sole authority, and the golden set is written from the requirement before the function to avoid the own-oracle trap.
- [[One-Sided Label Control]] — A labelling UI that can only write "wrong" makes precision unmeasurable by construction; pre-session checklist for label surfaces
- [[Outcome Signal Ladder]] — Interaction events are interest proxies; rank outcome signals by cost and strength and name the proxy limitation going in
